# Deliberate differences from GeoGebra

Behaviour that differs from GeoGebra on purpose is recorded here, with the
reason. A difference that is not listed here is a bug.

The family tables below cover recorded golden mismatches, verified t-pairs,
and file import gaps. The ledgers pin the exact failing outputs. Run
`python3 -I tools/check_gap_documentation.py` to check that every ledger
entry has a row.

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Objects created by a command with several results (`Tangent`, `AngularBisector` of two lines; `Focus`, `Vertex`, `Directrix` and `Tangent(P, conic)` of a conic) | The set of labelled objects can change over time: a result that is undefined when the command runs gets its label only once it becomes defined. | The objects are fixed when the command runs. All results that are defined at that moment are created (`t_{1}`, `t_{2}`); when at most one is defined, only that one (or the first) is created under the plain name. For `Focus`, `Vertex`, `Directrix` and `Tangent(P, conic)` the type of the conic when the command runs decides the set: a conic that is degenerate or of another type then (a hyperbola has two defined vertices of four, a parabola one focus and one directrix, a point on the conic one tangent) keeps that set when it later changes type. | A construction whose list of objects depends on the history of moves cannot be replayed or saved reliably. |
| `ClosestPoint(vector, point)` and `ClosestPoint` of two segments or rays | `GeoVector` is a `Path` in GeoGebra (source: `GeoVector implements Path`), so a vector is accepted. Two segments or rays are `GeoLine`s and give `AlgoClosestPointLines`. | Rejected with an error. Only two lines are supported (their intersection, as `AlgoClosestPointLines` is `AlgoIntersectLines`). | The kernel stores no start point for a vector. Not observed with a golden case: a case script that the kernel cannot load fails the script corpus test, so these forms stay unverified. |
| `TriangleCenter`, `ClosestPointRegion` | Available. | Not implemented. | `TriangleCenter` needs the large `AlgoKimberlingWeights` table. |
| Objects created with a polygon (`Polygon(...)`, `Vertex(p)`, `InteriorAngles(p)`, `Angle(p)`) | At creation GeoGebra labels only the sides of `Polygon(A, B, C, ...)` that are defined then; for `Polygon(A, B, n)` nothing besides the polygon when `round(n) <= 2`, and no sides when `A` or `B` is undefined (all observed with the harness). Later, `AlgoPolygonRegularND.updateOutput` creates and labels more vertices and sides when `n` grows past the number it has; `Vertex(p)` and the angle commands create more outputs when `p` gets more vertices (and `Vertex(p)` of a polygon that is undefined at creation starts with one). Whether an unlabelled side is labelled once it becomes defined is not recorded: the harness only sees the labels given at creation. | The objects are fixed when the command runs and follow the labelling at creation: the sides of `Polygon(A, B, C, ...)` that are defined then; for `Polygon(A, B, n)` nothing besides the polygon when `round(n) <= 2`, else every new vertex, and the sides only when `A` and `B` are defined then. The polygon itself still has all `round(n)` vertices (`Construction::vertices`), and its area, perimeter and centroid use them; only the extra point, segment and angle objects are missing. | Same as for commands with several results: the list of objects must not depend on the history of moves. |
| Regular polygon with very many vertices | No limit on `n`. | `Polygon(A, B, n)` with `round(n)` above 1000 is refused when it is created, and the polygon is undefined while `round(n)` is above 1000 later. Otherwise the vertex count follows GeoGebra's `(int) Math.round(n)`, including its wrapping (`4294967299` gives 3 vertices) and `NaN` counting as 2. | A regular polygon creates `n` objects and computes `n` vertices on every update; a stray large number would stall the application. |
| Polyline through a point at infinity | The point counts as defined, so the length is not finite. | The point leaves a hole and the length stays finite. | Polyline vertices are read as Cartesian coordinates. |
| Arguments of the polygon commands (`Polygon`, `PolyLine`, `Area`, `Perimeter`, `Centroid`, `Vertex`, `InteriorAngles`, `Angle` of a polygon or polyline) | Any expression, such as `Polygon(A, B, n + 1)`, `Vertex(p, 2 * k)` or `Polygon((0, 0), (1, 0), (0, 1))`. | Each argument is the label of an existing object or, for a number, a plain decimal literal such as `5`, `-2` or `3.5` (no exponent, sign `+` or words such as `inf`); an existing label always wins. | The script format has no expression parser for these commands; a definition must refer to objects. |
| Creation-time flag of a circle intersection (`special` in `Def::IntersectCircles`) | `AlgoIntersectConics` decides when the intersection is created, from the positions at that moment, whether a point on both circles by construction is intersection 1, and keeps the decision. | The same; a rebuild from the definitions keeps the flag. A script cannot state it, so running the script with the free objects elsewhere can decide it otherwise and give other values. | The script syntax follows GeoGebra's commands, which have no such argument. |
| Elementary functions in expressions (`sin(a°)`, `gamma`, `acosh`, `round(a, 2)`) | Some values come from BigDecimal arithmetic (multiples of degrees in `sin`, `cos`, `tan`) or from Apache Commons Math (gamma, some inverse functions). | Plain `f64` arithmetic, Rust's standard functions and an own Lanczos gamma. `round(a, n)` of a value with an exact decimal (see the next row) uses that decimal; otherwise it rounds the shortest decimal form of `a` half up. | No dependencies, and Apache Commons Math is not GeoGebra code. The results differ at most in the last bits (well inside the golden tolerance), e.g. `sin(30°)` is `0.49999999999999994` instead of `0.5`. |
| Segments as numbers (`segmentvalue-contexts-001` to `-012`) | A segment is a `NumberValue`: wherever an expression reads it as a number, it is its length, also in `Sum` of a list of segments. | A segment is its length as an operand of arithmetic, a comparison or a function (`2s`, `s / t`, `s < t`, `sqrt(s)`, `f(x) = s x`); `==` and `!=` of two objects that are not numbers, points, vectors or Booleans compare them as `isEqualExtended` does, and such an object is never equal to a number (`segmentvalue-objects-001` to `-007`). Polygons, polylines, arcs and sectors are number values in the reference (area or length), which `==` compares first; the kernel has no such number in an expression, so `==` and `!=` with one of them stay refused, as before. A list literal of segments stays a list of objects, so `Sum({s, t})` is refused; the reference gives the sum of the lengths. The case is not recorded, because every recorded script must load in the kernel. | Typing a list literal of segments as numbers would change how existing lists of segments are kept and drawn. |
| Juxtaposed names such as `ab` when `a` and `b` exist | Looks up the whole name first, then resolves concatenated variables and object labels. | Looks up the whole name first. In ordinary expressions, unresolved names of up to 64 bytes can split into single lowercase letters or pi when each part resolves. Equation variables use their existing rules. | The bounded fallback covers single-letter products. Segmentation into several multi-letter names needs a separate resolver. |
| Work of one exact decimal operation (`expr-decimal-027`) | `BigDecimal` arithmetic has no limit: `(1E-100001 + 1) - 1 + 0.1 + 0.2` is exactly `0.3`. Exponents outside the `int` scale throw (`(1E-999999999)^3`: "Underflow"; `1E-3000000000`: "Exponent overflow"), and a product such as `1E-999999999 1E-999999999` runs out of memory; each line is rejected. | The same arithmetic while one operation stays within bounds: a result of at most 100000 digits, a product or division of at most 10^7 digit products, and an exponent within Java's `int` range. An operation past a bound, and every operation after it in the same expression, is computed with doubles: the chain above with `1E-100001` gives `0.30000000000000004` (with `1E-5001` it gives `0.3`, as GeoGebra). The lines GeoGebra rejects for their exponent give the double result (`0`) instead of an error. | A hostile line must not use unbounded memory or time on every update. `expr-decimal-027` needs 100002 digits to align `1E-100001` with `1`. The binary64 rounding residual becomes `5551.115123125783` after multiplication by `1E20`. GeoGebra applies `BigDecimal.add` and `BigDecimal.subtract` in `DoubleOperation.java:31,42`, so its exact result rounds to `0`. |
| Exact decimal of a free number that was set (`expr-exact-037`) | A number made from a decimal literal keeps the literal's decimal for exact arithmetic until its value is set (`GeoNumeric.setValue`, a drag, a slider), even to the same value. So the last bits of what reads it depend on whether it was ever set. | The same. `Construction::expression` hands out a free number's decimal, so a rebuild through the API keeps it. A script line states only the value: replaying a construction whose number was set gives the literal's decimal back and can change dependents in the last bits. | Same as the circle intersection flag: the script syntax follows GeoGebra's, which has no way to say that a number was set. The property tests compare against a first `set` of the number for this reason. |
| Expression syntax that is not supported | Degrees-minutes-seconds input (`30°15'`), gradians and `rad`, calls with square brackets (`sin[x]`), `xcoord(` and `ycoord(`, points written `(1 \| 2)`, comparisons, booleans, `¬` and `If`, complex numbers, functions beyond the built-in list, `1..2` as a list, `random()`. | Each is an error, and the line leaves nothing behind. | Not implemented. Comparisons and `If` need a boolean value kind and an undefined branch; `random()` cannot be checked against golden data. |
| Digits other than `0`-`9` | The parser takes digits of other scripts (Arabic-Indic, Devanagari, ...) in numbers. | Only ASCII digits make a number; other digits are an error. | Rare in practice, and the literal text is also the exact decimal, which needs ASCII digits. |
| `inf`, `Infinity`, `NaN` and `Undefined` as labels | Read as the constants ∞ and undefined, so they cannot be labels. | Accepted as labels. In an expression the name is still the constant; in a polygon command an existing label wins. | The polygon argument forms read plain labels and depend on this. |
| `SolveODE` and `NSolveODE` with an infinite start, end or step | The infinite number is a defined value and is handed to the fixed-step integrator as it is; nothing bounds the number of steps. | The locus is undefined. | Every evaluation has a work budget; an integration towards an infinite end has no result to agree with. |
| `Sum` or `Product` over a range of an expression that does not read the variable (`Sum(u, k, 1, 3)`) | Updating the variable never reaches the expression and the update recurses until the stack overflows (`AlgoFoldExpression.updateLocalVar`). | The fold is computed: the constant term is added or multiplied once per step. | There is no reference result to agree with. |
| Repeated assignments | Replaces a definition, reorders its dependencies as needed, and can replace objects that own multiple outputs. | Keeps the object's ID for single-output scalar, expression, coordinate, and point-on-path definitions. A replacement can read later objects if the dependency graph stays acyclic. A kind change requires an object without dependents. Replacements that create auxiliary objects or several outputs are refused. | Existing references retain their IDs and expected kinds. Graph reordering is bounded and runs when a replacement needs a new dependency order. |

## Opening construction files (`geo-import`)

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `$point(x, y)` in the script language | The parser's `$point` follows the label rule: with a lower-case label the result is a vector. The XML expresses a lower-case point with `type="point"` on the `<expression>`. | `a = $point(1, 2)` is a free point whatever the label. `$vector(x, y)` is a free vector whatever the label, as in GeoGebra. | A saved file has lower-case points and upper-case vectors, and the script has to reproduce the right kind. |
| Which outputs a command creates, in a saved file | Labelled outputs are those that were defined when the command ran. | `{t_{1}, t_{2}} = Tangent(P, k)` creates exactly the outputs that have a label; an empty slot creates nothing. When both tangents from `P` are the same line at creation (`P` on the circle) the kernel still makes only the first, so a label for the second is an error and a file saved with both labels does not import that command. | The file states the labels, so the kernel does not decide. The limit is in `tangent_points` (`algo/lines.rs`). |
| `Distance(line, line)` in a file | `AlgoDistanceLineLine` computes in call order and stores the reverse order. The raw parallelism test can treat lines with coefficients near 1e-6 as parallel, and their computed distance depends on input order. The cached value is the pre-save result. | Export reverses the input order. Import recomputes in file order and accepts the numeric cache if it matches either that result or the result in the pre-save order. Values matching neither order are reported. | The four files in `distance-reload.snapshot` have five affected labels. The reloaded values are checked against the Java reader and the original construction values stay in the golden corpus. Resaving again reverses the order again, as in the reference. |
| Unindexed `Intersect` in a file | GeoGebra keeps the permutation chosen by the first update. After a reversal the two saved labels can be in the opposite order from a fresh computation. | For a saved pair with two distinct points, reverse the indices when both saved coordinates match the opposite slots at the existing tolerance. All 24 `saved-firstmove` files agree after import. | The coordinates disambiguate the branch labels at load. Later edits still use the indexed kernel solver. |
| Undo and redo of conic intersections | A restored intersection can recover its branch assignment from its saved points. | Undo and redo rebuild the conic intersection from its script and replay the retained edits. A branch can change after replay if the original assignment depended on an intermediate position. | The workbench log coalesces edits and stores neither intermediate positions nor the conic pair's slot permutation and candidate history. Rebuilding from kernel definitions can carry the compact permutation. |
| Labelled second point at a tangent point | `avoidDoubleTangentPoint` retains the duplicate when the second point has a label. | The indexed circle intersection retains only the first point at tangency. The 12 cases `saved-doubletangent-001` through `saved-doubletangent-012` report only `P_{2}` as undefined. | Restoring the duplicate requires carrying the unindexed command’s labelled-output policy into the indexed solver. Each case is listed in `known-import-gaps.toml`. |
| Segments, rays, polygons in the saved-value check | A segment or ray is saved as its line only; a polygon or polyline has no saved value at all. | Lines are compared (up to a positive factor); the end points are the objects the segment was made from, and are compared as such. Polygons are not compared against the file. | Nothing else is in the file. |
| Circle radius in the saved-value check | The matrix `A0..A5` is saved, and the radius is only recoverable as a difference of terms of size about `cx^2 + cy^2`. | Centre compared at 1e-9; the squared radius at 40 * 2.2e-16 * (`cx^2 + cy^2 + |A2/A0|`) or the radius at 1e-9, whichever is looser. | The bound comes from the rounding of the matrix itself (about ten roundings of terms of that size, times four), not from 1e-9 of the squared terms. |
| Value saved in the file vs value computed | The file holds the values of the moment it was saved. | The kernel recomputes; an object whose cache differs by more than `1e-9` relative is kept and listed in `Imported::mismatched` unless the line-distance writer rule above explains the cache (the CLI prints it, the app counts it). | A disagreement is information, not a reason to drop an object. |
| Labels of polygon outputs | The file names the polygon, its sides and new vertices. | The kernel labels them itself (as GeoGebra does); a file label with no counterpart (a renamed side) is skipped with its dependents. | The kernel has no way to name the outputs of `Polygon`. |
| Buttons, checkboxes and input boxes (`button`, `boolean`, `textfield`) | Controls appear in the graphics view. Input boxes edit or redefine their linked object. | Free checkboxes use their saved canvas position and caption and stay editable in the algebra sidebar when hidden. The checkbox tool creates a free checkbox with an optional caption. Input boxes show their caption and linked value on the canvas. They edit free numbers, angles, Booleans and text. | The checkbox tool has no dialog for visibility conditions. Buttons have no canvas control. Unlinked text entry and redefinition of linked objects are outside the editor. The linked text preview uses at most 256 characters. Editing opens the full value up to 16,384 characters. Larger values stay unchanged to bound layout and undo work. |
| Consecutive pen gestures (`canvas-reviewstrokes-001`) | Two consecutive gestures in the probe produce one pen object containing both paths. | Each gesture creates a separate pen object and undo step, including when both event sequences arrive in one frame. At most nine gestures finish in one frame and each uses at most 512 input samples. | The editor records each gesture as an independent construction action. Timed pen-object grouping is outside this round. |
| Desktop list and locus witnesses (`canvas-reviewchart-001`, `canvas-reviewbounds-001`, `canvas-reviewlocus-001`) | The reference picks the first dot of all 17 charts and the 257-member plot, the final point of the third list, and the edge beyond the first 10,001 locus samples. | The desktop list traversal allows 256 entries per list and 4,096 across a pointer query. Camera fitting and list painting share 512 per frame. A stationary pointer over blank canvas still triggers a separate hover query, which can read all 768 members of the three lists before painting. Desktop locus painting and picking use only the first 10,001 samples. | The limits bound the amount of expansion that a construction can require in a frame or pointer query. |
| Create List rectangle (`canvas-reviewrectangle-001`) | The polyline and point in the probe create no list. | The operation adds no undo step when shape preparation, containment and selection filtering exceed their combined 4,096-unit limit. It also stops on shapes that require a fresh clipping overlay. | The rectangle query can use computed contours and cannot trigger expensive geometry recomputation. |
| Transformed polygons in a Create List rectangle (`canvas-reviewstretch-001`) | The rectangle creates a list with the original polygon, all 70 copies and all 71 vectors. | Rectangle selection charges one unit per vector visited when it resolves the axis start of each copy. This rectangle exhausts the shared 4,096-unit budget and creates no list or undo step. | The budget bounds repeated traversal of shared dependencies. |
| Object scripts on any saved element | `GeoElement.getScriptTags` writes GeoGebra Script or JavaScript for click, update, drag-end and change events. | The import and snapshot preserve the script text and event as inert data. A file with object scripts gets an import note. | The application does not execute imported scripts. |
| `<condition showObject>` | A boolean label or expression controls visibility, independently of the saved show flag. | The condition is restored after the objects it reads. The workbench and imported SVG use its boolean value. | Visibility is a presentation property and may read an object defined later in the file. |
| Checkbox caption substitutions | A caption can substitute values from the checkbox or read the first-row cell in the next spreadsheet column. | Boolean, numeric, and text values are substituted in the graphics view and SVG. Other kinds in the adjacent cell use their label. An expansion above 16,384 bytes uses the literal caption. | General spreadsheet value formatting is outside the canvas controls. The cap bounds the per-caption substitution work on each frame. |
| Saved 2D element types | `GeoElement.getElementOpenTagXML` uses the XML names in `GeoClass`. It writes `image`, `implicitpoly`, `polyline`, `locus`, `curvecartesian` and `numeric` with `<slider>`, among other 2D types. | Implicit polynomial equations and their coefficient matrices load as implicit elements. Polylines and loci rebuild from their commands, and `CurveCartesian(number, number, number, number, number)` rebuilds from its two coordinate expressions, local parameter and bounds. Free numeric sliders restore literal, expression-valued and one-sided bounds. The imported value and later edits are clamped to the applicable bounds. Missing or unsupported bounds produce an import note. | The canvas uses saved slider placement, knob size and increment. A slider needs two finite bounds to draw its knob. The kernel has no drawable turtle or media object. Audio, video and embeds are outside scope because playback and hosted content are outside the geometry kernel. |
| Limits | None. | Unpacked XML at most 256 MiB, at most 4096 zip entries, at most 200000 items; beyond them an error. Only stored and deflated entries, no zip64. | Hostile input. |
| Constant expressions | An `<expression>` with no object in it is an independent object. | Read as a free object with the saved value; any identifier makes it an ordinary expression, which is skipped. | Same rule as GeoGebra's `isIndependent`. |
| Outputs that are undefined when a command runs, in a saved file | Have no label in the file. | Cannot be recovered: the importer reports them as skipped without a label. | The label is not in the file. |
| Expression syntax that is not supported (supersedes the row above of the same name) | Degrees-minutes-seconds input (`30°15'`), gradians and `rad`, calls with square brackets (`sin[x]`), `xcoord(` and `ycoord(`, points written `(1 \| 2)`, complex numbers, functions beyond the built-in list, `1..2` as a list, `random()`; lists compared (`{1, 2} == {1, 2}`) or combined with logic, lists in functions (`f(x) = {x, 1}`), lists as branches of `If`, a list as a coordinate (`(l, 1)`), `Sum(l, f)` with a list of frequencies, `Zip` and the other list commands not named here. | Each is an error, and the line leaves nothing behind. Comparisons, booleans (`true`, `!`, `¬`, `&&`, `\|\|`, `⊕`, `->`), `If`, list literals, element access `l(n)`, `Element`, `Length`, `Sum`, `Product`, `Min`, `Max`, `Mean`, `Reverse`, `First`, `Last`, `Sort`, `Append`, `Join` and `Sequence` are supported. | Not implemented yet; `random()` cannot be checked against golden data. |
| Constant booleans and lists (`t = true`, `l = {1, 2, 3}`) | Independent objects, with a checkbox for the boolean and an editable list. | An imported free boolean can be edited through its checkbox. Typed literal booleans and lists use the expression engine. | The checkbox is an editable definition with a Boolean result. |
| Very long lists | No limit on the number of elements. | A list whose evaluation needs more than 2^20 elements, nested ones included, is undefined while that holds (`Sequence(2000000)`). | A stray large count would stall the application and exhaust memory on every recompute. |
| `Sort` of values closer than `1e-8` | A `TreeSet` with a comparator that treats values within `1e-8` as equal (and points with such `x` by `y`), so the order of near ties depends on the insertion order and the shape of the tree. | Numbers and booleans by exact value, points by exact `x`, then `y`; undefined elements last. | The comparator is not a total order and cannot be reproduced by a sort; the difference is limited to elements within `1e-8` of each other. |
| Expression syntax that is not supported (supersedes the rows above of the same name) | Degrees-minutes-seconds input (`30°15'`), gradians and `rad`, calls with square brackets (`sin[x]`), `xcoord(` and `ycoord(`, points written `(1 \| 2)`, complex numbers, functions beyond the built-in list, `1..2` as a list, `random()`; lists compared (`{1, 2} == {1, 2}`) or combined with logic, lists in functions (`f(x) = {x, 1}`), `Sum(l, f)` with a list of frequencies; `Last` of a function (only a hand-drawn line graph fits); `Union`, `Intersection` and `Remove` with polygons (also a polygon and a list of points); `RandomElement`, `SelectedElement` and `SelectedIndex`. | Each is an error, and the line leaves nothing behind. Comparisons, booleans (`true`, `!`, `¬`, `&&`, `\|\|`, `⊕`, `->`), `If` (also with list branches), list literals, Cartesian tuples with lists as coordinates, element access `l(n)`, `Element`, `Length`, `Sum`, `Product` (also with a list of frequencies), `Min`, `Max`, `Mean`, `Reverse`, `First` (also of a locus), `Last`, `Sort` (also with a list of keys), `Append`, `Insert`, `Take`, `Remove`, `RemoveUndefined`, `Unique`, `Flatten`, `IndexOf`, `Frequency`, `Classes`, `Zip`, `Join`, `Sequence`, `PointList`, `OrdinalRank`, `TiedRank`, `Union`, `Intersection` and `Remove` of lists are supported, and the text forms of `First`, `Last`, `Take`, `Element`, `IndexOf`, `Length`, `Sort`, `Unique` and `Frequency` as well. | Not implemented yet; `random()` cannot be checked against golden data. |
| An out-of-range element of a list of lists (`l(n)`) inside an expression | The helper list is a `GeoList` that `setUndefined` does not clear: it keeps the elements it last held (or a template element, a list holding one undefined number), each set undefined, and what reads it element by element gives a defined list of undefined elements (`Length` of it counts them). | The list is undefined, and so is what reads it. Harness cases `lists-index-017`, `lists-objects-025`, and `lists-objects-026` are known gaps. | Element selection still lacks the retained template used in these cases. |
| Very long lists (supersedes the row above of the same name) | No limit on the number of elements or on the work of one update: `AlgoSequence` builds as many elements as its range gives and stops only when free memory is critical, and is undefined only when the count `(to - from) / step` is infinite; `AlgoSequenceRange` is undefined only past `MyMath.LARGEST_INTEGER` or with a zero step or one of the wrong sign. A step that cannot reach the end (within `Kernel.MIN_PRECISION` of it, or too small to change the variable) never ends. | One evaluation of one object may build at most 2^20 elements in all, across every nesting level and counting lists that are built and dropped again (an inner `Sequence` for each value of an outer one, the lists a `Sum`, `Length` or `First` reads in a loop). The count of a range stops at what is left of that bound. When the bound is crossed, the evaluation stops at once and the object is undefined while that holds (`Sequence(2000000)`, `Sequence(Sequence(j + k, j, 1, k), k, 1, 1E5)`, `Sequence(j, j, 0, 1, 1E-300)`). | A stray large count, or a nested `Sequence` whose total grows with the square of its range, would stall the application on every recompute. |
| Very long lists and costly evaluations (supersedes the rows above named "Very long lists") | No limit on the number of elements or on the work of one update. `AlgoSequence` builds as many elements as its range gives and stops only when free memory is critical; a function applied for each value costs its whole tree each time. `AlgoSequenceRange` gives an undefined list past `MyMath.LARGEST_INTEGER` or with a zero step or one of the wrong sign, and `AlgoSequence` one for an infinite count `(to - from) / step`; such a list inside another is an undefined element. `Sequence(n)` counts to `(int) Math.round(n)`, so a count past `int` wraps and one of 0 or less is empty. A step that cannot reach the end (within `Kernel.MIN_PRECISION` of it, or too small to change the variable) never ends. | The ranges GeoGebra refuses give the same results (golden cases `lists-limit-*`): an undefined list, an undefined element of a list around it (`{Sequence(1, 1E20), {1}}` is `{?, {1}}`, and `Sequence(Sequence(1, 1E20), k, 1, 1E6)` a million undefined elements, built and counted like any others), and `Sequence(n)` wraps like `(int)` (`Sequence(1E20)` is `{}`, `Sequence(2^32 + 3)` is `{1, 2, 3}`). Beyond that, one evaluation of one object has two bounds of its own, and past either it stops at once and the whole object is undefined, not only the list being built: at most 2^20 elements built in all, nested lists and lists built and dropped again included (memory: `Sequence(2000000)`, `Sequence(Sequence(j + k, j, 1, k), k, 1, 1E5)`, `Sequence(j, j, 0, 1, 1E-300)`), and at most 2^25 units of work (time). A unit is about one node of a tree: the tree of the object once (functions expanded), the body of a `Sequence` again for each value of its variable, a step of the list code (counting, filling, copying or reading an element), and in exact decimal arithmetic on numbers too long for the inline forms (77 digits) a digit handled or four digit products. A list of the full 2^20 elements fits with a body of a cost up to 29 (`Sequence(k + k + ... + k, k, 1, 1048576)` with 15 terms); a function of a cost of about 280 000 applied for each of 10 000 values does not. | A stray large count, a nested `Sequence` whose total grows with the square of its range, or a costly function applied for every value would stall the application on every recompute. |
| Very long lists and costly evaluations: the work charged (supersedes the row above named "Very long lists and costly evaluations") | As in the row above: no limit on the number of elements or on the work of one update. | The ranges GeoGebra refuses give the same results, and each evaluation budget allows 2^20 elements (see the row above). The bound on work charges what the evaluation does, as it does it: at most 80 000 000 units for one evaluation of one object with lists, about 0.3 s of a release build (a unit is about 3.5 ns, measured; the slowest evaluations near the bound take 0.24 to 0.38 s: 0.36 s for quotients by a 3 000-digit number without zero digits and 0.38 s for `Max(L) + k` over a list object of 10 000 elements, about 4.8 ns for each element read). A node visited is 3 units in the full evaluation and 1 in `evaluateDouble`, a power 8 more and a function of a number 4 more; ordinary expressions visit the selected `If` branch. Inline list helpers in inactive branches can update their retained shapes within a separate work and element budget shared by all such helpers. At construction, validation of list dilation in a conditional expression shares the evaluation allowance and rejects the command if validation exhausts it. A function sampled or expanded is charged its nodes at each call. A step of the list code (counting, filling, copying or reading an element) is a unit, a sort `n (1 + log2 n)`. Each exact decimal operation is charged its measured time: 3 units for a sum, product or rounding of coefficients up to 38 digits, 30 up to 77 digits, 100 for a quotient, 40 for the exact decimal of a double and 90 to 180 for the double of a decimal; on longer numbers 100 units and a unit for each digit handled (64 digits that are only scanned or copied whole), each 24 digit products of a product and each 2 of a quotient; digits without zeros were measured as well as numbers like `1 + 1E-3000`. Every node is checked before it runs and every loop of the list code before it starts, so an evaluation goes past either work budget by at most the exact operations of one node (at most four, 2 467 067 units in all, about 9 ms; at most 88 071 units in the cases measured). A list object read in a body (`L(k)`, `Element(L, k)`, `Length(L)`, `Max(L)`, `First(L, k)`) is read in place, as upstream reads the `GeoList` itself: it is neither copied nor counted against the element bound (each read was, so `L = Sequence(k^2, k, 1, n)` and `Sequence(L(k) + 1, k, 1, n)` were undefined from about n = 2 000); each element read is a unit of work, so a body that reads a whole list for each value is charged every element it reads: with `L = Sequence(k^2, k, 1, 10000)`, `Sequence(Max(L) + k, k, 1, 10000)` reads 10^8 elements and is undefined after 0.38 s (at 3 000 values it is defined); a list that holds such a list is copied and counted when it is stored. So ordinary lists are defined: `f(x) = 3.7 x (1 - x)` nested nine times at 10 000 values is about 1 million units, and `Sequence(1.0000001^999999 + k, k, 1, 10000)` 62 million (0.26 s); `Sequence(k * 1.0001^365, k, 1, n)` is undefined from about n = 31 700, the function `g` of about 240 000 nodes at 10 000 values stops after 0.27 s, and `Sequence(1 / 3 + 1 / 7 + 1 / 9 + 1 / 11 + 1 / 13 + 1 / 17 + 1 / 19, k, 1, 1048576)`, which took 7.4 s, is undefined after 0.29 s. The bound is per evaluation of one object: an update that recomputes several dependent objects may take up to that much for each of them. It covers objects with lists only: an object without lists is bounded only by the static cost of its tree and the caps of each exact operation, so a balanced sum of 2^18 copies of `1.0000001^999999` (a line of 4.8 MB) takes 7.6 s. | Charging a body its static cost for each value (both branches of an `If`, a sampled function for each call around it) charged ordinary lists 6 to 300 times their work and made them undefined, while short exact decimals were not charged at all; charging the work done keeps the bound a bound on time. |

## Numeric algebra and logic

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Relation(any, any)` and `Relation(list)` | Opens a dialog with numerical results and, where available, a link to the symbolic prover. | Stores the numerical findings in an English algebra row and updates them with the input objects. | The algebra row makes changes in the numerical result visible during a move. The symbolic prover is outside scope. |
| Work limit for prime factors, divisors and interval extrema | Runs the number-theory loop or Brent iterations each time its inputs change. | Charges the kernel work budget, including each sampled function evaluation, and gives an undefined result when the budget is exhausted. | Nested use shares the same budget as list construction. |
| `DivisorsSum` of a large square (`algebraedge-integer-001`, `algebraedge-integer-002`) | `AlgoDivisorsSum` multiplies `Math.round((Math.pow(p, e + 1) - 1) / (p - 1))` over the prime factors. For `n = 92285573^2` and `n = 63076583^2` the exact sums are 8516627076223903 and 3978655386032473, but `p^3` is near `8e23` and `2.5e23`, where one ulp of `Math.pow` moves the quotient by one or two. `Math.pow` may differ from `StrictMath.pow` by one ulp: the recorded files give 8516627076223902 and 3978655386032473 (the `StrictMath.pow` values), while HotSpot's x86_64 `Math.pow` intrinsic gives 8516627076223904 and 3978655386032474 under JDK 17 and JDK 21, in every run. Run with `-XX:-UseLibmIntrinsic`, the reference gives the recorded values again. | Computes `pow` with the fdlibm algorithm behind `StrictMath.pow` and agrees with the recorded values. | The reference result depends on the platform's `Math.pow`; the kernel follows one fixed, portable choice. These two cases are not re-recorded. |
| Out-of-range matrix calls | An invalid row index yields a list of undefined entries with the matrix's column count. | An invalid row index yields an undefined result. | List indexing shares the same out-of-range behavior for flat and nested lists. |

## General conics

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Conic(A, B, C, D, E)` through five points that lie on one parabola by construction | `AlgoConicFivePoints` checks the incidence lists of the points and, when all five lie on the same parabola, sets the tolerance of `det S = 0` to infinity so the result is always a parabola. | The tolerance is always `Kernel.MIN_PRECISION`, as for any other five points. | The kernel keeps no incidence lists. Free points or points from other commands are not affected. |
| Homogeneous coordinates of the points of `Conic` | The lines through pairs of points are cross products of the points' homogeneous coordinates, so a point from `Intersect` with `z` other than 1 gives other bits. | Every point has `z = 1`. | The kernel keeps points in Cartesian coordinates. The results differ in the last bits only. |
| Forms of the conic commands | `Conic` of a list or of six coefficients, `Ellipse` and `Hyperbola` with a segment as the axis length. | Refused with an error. | Not implemented. |
| State of a conic | A `GeoConic` keeps fields from earlier updates: the order of the two lines of a degenerate conic follows the old lines, and the half-axes or the eccentricity of a type that does not set them keep old values. | The same: the kernel keeps GeoGebra's whole record per conic object and computes the next one from it (`ConicData`). A construction rebuilt from its definitions starts from fresh records, so after moves the order of two lines can differ from the rebuilt one, as it would in GeoGebra after reopening a file. | Keeping the state is what makes the classification agree with GeoGebra's on degenerate conics. |

## Function intersections

| Area | GeoGebra | Geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Output growth of `Intersect(f, h, a, b)` | `AlgoRoots` allocates more points when an edit exposes more roots in the interval and labels them with the next default point labels (`A`, `B`, ...). `AlgoRoots.initPoints` removes every point from slot `max(n, 1)` on that no object reads once only `n` roots remain, which frees its label; with no root left and some point read by another object, it removes every other point, the first one included. When a slot has a root again `AlgoGeoPointsFunction.updateLabels` labels it: the first slot with the label typed before `=`, the others with the next default point label, which can be one another command freed. Commands update in construction order. If the typed label is taken, `GeoElement.setLabel` renames the object that holds it. A `.ggb` file saves a removed point as an empty output slot (`AlgoElement.getCmdOutputXML`). | The same removals and labels: a removed output stays in the construction without a label, and groups update in creation order. Growth and removal run after every edit of a parent: dragging, a typed assignment such as `b = 10`, and the replay of a script in the workbench, a file or the command line. A `$boundarySlot` line labels its slot, also a removed one; slot 1 names the first output by the label it had (`R = $boundarySlot(R_{1}, 1)`). During a replay, growth stops before a slot that the script labels with its own line. Rebuilds for undo and redo and the restore of a snapshot remove the outputs after the same edits; a set that removed outputs is kept in the journal rather than merged into the next set. If the typed label is taken, the first output stays without a label. A deletion rebuild leaves removals to the next update of the command, as the reference does when an output loses the object that read it; during that replay an undefined output is removed only when a later line of the journal takes its label. The journal it leaves closes that part with a `$keptLabels()` line, so redo, the undo of later edits, snapshots and saved scripts replay it the same way. The export refuses a construction with a removed output, as it refuses grown interval roots. `intersectgrowth-interval-001` to `-007` and `intersectgrowth-reuse-001` to `-004` agree. | Renaming another object to free the typed label is not recorded. The export does not write empty output slots, which the importer cannot read back. |
| Expression work for function intersections with another function, a line carrier or a conic | Numerical root methods bound their solver iterations and evaluate each sampled expression. | Fraction expansion, root search and evaluation share the kernel expression work budget for a root list. Outputs are undefined if the budget is exhausted. | The budget bounds nested function evaluation across all samples of a search. |

## Drawing conics and functions (`plot`, the window and the SVG writer)

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Function graphs in the graphics view | `DrawParametricCurve` plots a function with the `IntervalPlotter` (interval arithmetic) where it can, and with `CurvePlotter` otherwise. | Always the `CurvePlotter` algorithm (`plot::function`): bisection to 10 pixels and 10 degrees, jumps and undefined values break the path. | The kernel has no interval arithmetic. `CurvePlotter` is GeoGebra's own fallback and keeps poles, steps and gaps apart. |
| Shape of a conic | `DrawConic` hands Java2D an ellipse shape (clipped to an arc when larger than the view), a hyperbola sampled at 32 + 10 points per view width (at most 300) and a parabola as one cubic Bézier curve. | Polylines over the parameter ranges that lie on the view, sampled so that no chord is more than a quarter of a pixel off the curve (`plot::conic`). The thresholds past which nothing is drawn (`HUGE_RADIUS`) and the label positions are `DrawConic`'s. | One drawing path for the window (an `f32` painter) and the SVG writer, exact at every zoom. |
| Label of a degenerate conic | `DrawLine` places the labels of the lines. | The label sits in the middle of the first visible line. | The line label rule of `DrawLine` is not ported. |
| Label of a function graph in the window | `CurvePlotter` puts it at the first sample on screen. | The same once the view is still (the graph is then a plot of the area). While a kept plot is shown for another view (during a pan, a zoom or a resize), the label is the first drawn point inside the area. | The kept plot is moved by a pan without plotting again. |
| Redrawing after a pan, a zoom or a resize (window) | Every curve is plotted again for every new view. | A curve is plotted with a margin of a quarter of the area's longer side and moved by a pan; it is plotted again past half the margin. Inside the area a function's graph is a plot of the area alone; once the view is still, every function whose plot was made for another view is plotted again, so an idle window shows the graphs as a plot of the visible area gives them. A conic's polylines are plotted on the area with its margin, within a quarter of a pixel of the curve wherever the view is. After a view change at most 24 conics (or 6 functions, each counting as 4) are plotted per frame; the others are drawn for a few frames from their previous polylines moved and scaled (not at all past 4 times larger or smaller), and the window asks for frames until all are plotted. Adding, deleting, undoing, redoing or selecting an object plots only the curves whose drawing changed. A pan faster than the plotting keeps up with (about 20 px a frame with 300 curves) can leave a strip at the edge undrawn for a few frames. The SVG writer always plots afresh. | The frame budget of 4 ms with 300 curves on the view. |
| Redrawing during a pan (window) | Every curve is plotted again for every new view. | While the view pans at the same scale, a frame plots at most 12 conics (or 3 functions); the curves due again are drawn moved from their kept plot meanwhile, and once the view stops the full cap of 24 applies. A moved or scaled kept drawing keeps only the triangles of its stroke that reach the area. A kept drawing shown scaled keeps its corner joins, so a corner within float rounding of a right angle can stay mitred where a stroke of the scaled curve would cut it off, until the curve is plotted again. A fast pan therefore leaves strips at the edge undrawn a little longer than with the full cap (24 px a frame for 40 frames: 5535 curve draws past the margin, against 3762). | The 4 ms frame budget: a pan frame also moves every kept drawing. |
| Input box display and editing | Input boxes support symbolic editing, styles and linked redefinition. | The canvas editor changes linked free numbers, angles, Booleans and texts. It displays up to 256 characters and uses at most 100 columns to size the field. | Bounds the work of painting and picking an imported widget. |
| Cartesian curves and Boolean regions | Cartesian curves use the parametric plotter and inequalities draw their analytic borders with fills. | Cartesian curves sample a bounded set of parameters with the existing clipped curve cache. Boolean inequalities shade sampled cells on a grid of at most 64 by 64. The desktop draws up to two regions per frame and SVG draws up to eight. Each plot has a shared expression work limit. | Keeps work bounded for dense plots and costly expressions. |

## The view: pan, zoom and pinch (the window)

Each of these is a switch of `OwnerRules` (`crates/geo-app-core/src/app.rs`); the `REFERENCE` preset gives the GeoGebra column at run time, `OURS` (the default) the geokernel one. The recorded observations of both are asserted in `crates/geo-mcp/tests/replay.rs`.

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Undo steps of view changes (`OwnerRules::view_changes_are_steps`) | A run of wheel events adds no undo step while it runs: `CoordSystemStateForUndo` stores the view only at the next press on the canvas, or when the pointer leaves it. A pan, an axis drag, a zoom rectangle and a pinch store their step at the end of the gesture. | Every view change is an undo step right after the gesture that made it, a run of wheel events included. A view-only step never replays the journal, and the 500-step history evicts the oldest view-only step first, so sustained panning cannot crowd out construction steps. | The owner's decision (spec F3): an undo right after a zoom must return the view, whatever is pressed next. |
| Wheel zoom amount (`OwnerRules::wheel_zoom_fixed_per_event`) | One wheel event zooms by a fixed ×1.1 (×1.5 with Alt) whatever the scroll amount is; only the sign of the delta matters (`wrapMouseWheelMoved`). | The zoom is proportional to the scroll amount: ×1.1 per 100 points of it, one notch of a mouse wheel, so a slow trackpad roll zooms gently. | The owner's decision: a trackpad sends many small deltas, and a fixed jump per event overshoots. |
| Pinch zoom dead bands (`OwnerRules::pinch_bands`) | A two-finger pinch zooms only once the fingers' distance has changed by more than 10 px and pans only once their midpoint has moved more than 5 px (`MINIMAL_PIXEL_DIFFERENCE_FOR_ZOOM`, `MIN_MOVE` in `twoTouchMoveCommon`), so the view lags behind the fingers by the band. | No dead band: the view follows the fingers exactly, from their first move. | The owner's decision: what is under the fingers stays under the fingers. |
| Pan axis lock (`OwnerRules::pan_axis_lock`) | Within the first 40 px of a move-view drag, a move of more than 5 px within ±25° of an axis locks to that axis. The lock releases once displacement along the other axis exceeds 50 px (`SnapController`). | A move-view drag pans freely along both axes; there is no lock. | The owner's decision: a locked pan fights a user who means a diagonal. |
| Zoom limits (`OwnerRules::scale_limits`) | The view allows 1e-15 to 1e15 pixels per unit (`setCoordSystem`). | 1e-6 to 1e9: at 1e9 a unit spans a billion points, far past any screen, and below 1e-6 the drawing's `f32` meshes stop resolving the plane. | The owner's bounds (spec D15). |

## Points on paths, arcs and sectors

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Start position of `Point(path)` and `Point(path, u)` | The point is put at `(0, 0)`, then `(1, 0)` if it is undefined there, then at `(random(), 0)` (`AlgoDispatcher.point`). | `(0, 0)`, then `(1, 0)`; no random position. The point stays undefined when both fail. | A construction must be reproducible; a random start cannot be replayed or checked against golden data. |
| A point on a path moved to, or projected onto, a point at infinity | The point is a defined point at infinity, and `PathParameter` of it has a value. | The point is undefined, and so is its parameter; it comes back with its kept parameter when the path is finite again. | Points created by path placement keep a path record and are undefined in the Cartesian value when the position is at infinity. |
| `PathParameter` of a `ClosestPoint` | `AlgoClosestPoint` keeps the path parameter of its point between updates. A circle keeps the previous angle when the input produces a NaN angle and starts with angle zero. | `ClosestPoint` uses the retained circle angle to compute its position. `PathParameter` independently projects the original input and returns undefined if that input has no Cartesian position. | `PathParameter` uses the shared Cartesian path projection for its supported paths. |
| Arc of two rays and sector of a degenerate circumcircle | The arc's value is `∞` and counts as defined; the sector's area is not finite. | Both are undefined, as the golden data records them. | The kernel has no infinite object values. The part is still a path: points on it move along the rays or the segment. |
| Work budget for tangents to functions and lengths of their graphs | Evaluates a derivative expression. Numerical length also has a quadrature budget. | Reads the function's expression and evaluates the derivative at each sample within the kernel's expression work budget, shared across the samples of one length calculation. The result is undefined if this budget is exhausted. | Bounds the work even when a function applies other functions. |
| Forms not supported | `Arc(conic, P, Q)` and `Sector(conic, P, Q)` of a general conic (an object of `Conic`, `Ellipse`, ...; between two parameters it is supported); points on a part of one (`Point(a)`, `Point(a, u)` with `a = Arc(conic, s, t)` or `Sector(conic, s, t)`); an arc or sector as the input of `Center`, `Radius`, `Tangent` or a transformation. | Refused with an error. | Not implemented. Points on a general conic (`Point(conic)`, `Point(conic, u)`) are supported, by the conic's own parameterization. |

| The start point a locus freezes on a line without one, when the locus goes | `PathMoverLine.init` installs the foot from the origin on the line itself (`setStandardStartPoint`); deleting the locus leaves it installed, so the parameters of points on the line keep measuring from it. Undo and reloading a file do not save the point, so the next locus computation installs a fresh one on the line as it is then. | Deleting the locus keeps the frozen start: the deletion writes it into the journal where the locus entry was, so every replay — any number of later deletions, and the undo and redo of them — installs it at the same place, and moves recorded before it replay from the live foot of the line and moves after it from the frozen start, exactly as the construction being replaced had, bit for bit. A saved script does not include the entry: reloading drops the start, and the next locus computation installs a fresh one on the line as it is then, the same as GeoGebra's reload. An undo back to a state whose locus entry is still in the journal replays the locus line, which freezes the start as the replay computes it. | The frozen start is state of the line, not of the locus; the kernel keeps it in the construction, so the journal has to carry it over a rebuild explicitly (GeoGebra keeps it on the line object). |

## List commands

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Descriptive statistics of separate points or a freehand function | `CmdOneListFunction` wraps separate 2D points or the sampled freehand function into a point list. The numeric algorithms cannot use those coordinates as numbers. For `Mode`, `GeometricMean`, `HarmonicMean` and `RootMeanSquare` these forms produce typed undefined results. | These statistics accept lists and separate numbers. Separate points and freehand functions produce command errors. A single list containing points produces a typed undefined result for the four commands, as in the reference. | The four commands cover one list or at least two plain numbers. |
| Descriptive statistics on lists of angles and other geometric magnitudes | `AlgoStats1D` creates an unbounded angle if the list starts with an angle. GeoGebra also reads some geometric objects as numeric values in a list, such as segments by length. | The `AlgoStats1D` ports use the kernel's numeric list elements and return scalars. `Mean` refuses a single list typed as nonnumeric. `Mode`, `GeometricMean`, `HarmonicMean` and `RootMeanSquare` read numbers, angle radians, booleans and segment lengths and return numbers (a list of numbers for `Mode`). | Separate-argument wrapping requires plain numbers. Rejection cases are recorded in the `statistics-variadictypes` and `statistics-nextreject` fixtures. The four single-list commands refuse a second list. |
| Quartiles with unequal value and frequency list lengths and grouped medians with no qualifying class | The quartile computation skips unequal lengths. Grouped median leaves the result alone if no class reaches its target count. Both start at zero and can retain a prior result after an edit. | The result is zero when the reference skips assignment, as in a fresh construction. The quartile and median input checks still produce undefined results when they fail. | Recomputing from the current inputs keeps the result independent of edit history. |
| Very large frequency counts in median and quartiles | Each count converts to a signed 32-bit integer. The sum of counts, and intermediate quartile index arithmetic, can overflow. | Counts convert the same way. Cumulative counts and rank indices use 64-bit integers. Sorting and scanning are charged by input rows, including repeated rows. | Frequency counts cannot force materialization of the expanded samples or wrap the rank arithmetic. |
| `Sxx` and `SigmaXX` list scope and empty inputs | Both one-list dispatchers inspect the first item and throw on an initially empty list. When an existing input list becomes empty, `Sxx` is undefined and `SigmaXX` is zero. | Both commands take one numeric-compatible list, one list of 2D points, or two numeric-compatible lists. `Sxx` type-checks the equal-length Y list and computes the centered sum of squares of X. `SigmaXX` uses fractional frequencies for values or class midpoints and returns the uncentered sum of squares. For a defined empty list, `Sxx` is undefined and `SigmaXX` is zero, including at creation. | The result depends on the current list contents. List scans are charged per input row. Separate arguments and freehand functions are outside this slice. |
| `Sort` of texts | `GeoText.getComparator`: accent-insensitively (`StringUtil.removeAccents`, US lowercase, NFD, combining marks stripped), then the plain string, then the construction index; `Sort({"é", "e", "È", "Z", "a"})` is `{a, e, È, é, Z}`. | The same two comparisons, in Java's UTF-16 unit order; equal strings keep the order of the list. | Ported. The construction indexes of the elements of one list tie, so the comparator answers `+1` for two of them and the `TreeSet` puts the later one to the right: a stable sort matches it (`Sort({30, 10, 20}, {2, 1, 2})` is `{10, 30, 20}`). |
| Keys of one list within `1e-8` of each other | `GeoNumeric.getComparator` and `GeoPoint.getComparatorX` compare `a - b` and call a difference inside `DoubleUtil.isZero`'s `1e-8` a tie, so two unequal keys that near order by the tie answer, not by value: `Sort({2.800000000000001, 2.8})` keeps the order of the list. | The keys compare exactly, so the port orders such keys by value: `Sort({2.800000000000001, 2.8})` is `{2.8, 2.800000000000001}`. | The subtraction tolerance is not a transitive equality, and which side the tie answer sends an element to depends on the shape of GeoGebra's tree at that moment; a comparison sort cannot reproduce it. Equal keys, the case a script can name, keep the order of the list on both sides. |
| `Unique` and `Frequency` of texts | `new Frequency()`: exact strings, the counts in natural string order. | The same, one ordered bucket per string (Java's UTF-16 unit order). | Ported like for numbers. |
| Texts as list elements | Every command that compares, seeks, removes or counts elements takes texts: `IndexOf`, `Remove`, `Union`, `Intersection`, `Append`, `Insert`, `Element`, `First`, `Last`, `Take`, `Reverse`, `Frequency`, `Sort`, `Unique`. | The same, texts comparing by their string (`GeoText.isEqualExtended`). | Ported with the text kind. |
| `First(text)`, `First(text, number)`, `Last(text)`, `Last(text, number)`, `Element(text, number)` | A text goes in and a text comes out: `First("hello", 2)` is `he`, `Element("hello", 2)` is `e` (one UTF-16 unit). | The same (`AlgoFirstString`, `AlgoLastString`, `AlgoTextElement`), counting UTF-16 units like Java. | Ported with the text kind. |
| `Last(function)` | Gives the last point of the data of a function; only a hand-drawn line graph has data (`Operation.DATA`), so the form cannot be tried from a script with any other function either. | Refused with an error. | Hand-drawn line graphs live in the input view; the kernel has no way to create one, so this form has no path into it. |
| `Union(polygon, polygon)` | `AlgoPolygonUnion` of the Clipper library gives the polygon union with its boundary pieces. | Refused with an error. | Clipper is not ported; `Union` stays with lists. |
| `Sequence(any, number, number, number)` and `Sequence(any, number, number, number, number)` | The expression is evaluated for every value of the variable, which may hold every kind of value. | The same walk over every kind the kernel has, inside the same bounds as the other list forms. | The wildcard stands for the expression, which no single golden case can show for every kind. |
| `Zip(any, any, list, ...)` | The expression is evaluated for every tuple of the elements of the lists, and it may give every kind; lists of unequal length stop the walk at the shortest one. | The same walk for every kind the kernel has (`Zip(t, t, {{1}, {2}})` walks a list of lists). | The wildcard stands for the expression and its variables, which no single golden case can show. |
| `IndexOf(any, list)` and `IndexOf(any, list, number)` | Every kind of object can be sought. | Numbers, booleans, points, texts and lists are sought, a list element by element. | The wildcard stands for any object, which no single golden case can show. |
| `Insert(any, list, number)` | Every kind of object can be inserted; a list inserts its elements one by one. | Every kind the kernel has. | The wildcard stands for any object, which no single golden case can show. |
| `Append(list, any)` and `Append(any, list)` | Every kind of object can be appended or prepended. | Every kind the kernel has. | The wildcard stands for any object, which no single golden case can show. |
| `Product(any, number, number, number)` | `AlgoFoldExpression` evaluates the expression at `from` and folds it up to `(int) to`; a free variable in it, besides the fold variable, makes the result a function of that variable. | The same fold over numbers, its `(int)` casts wrapping like Java's; a free variable in the expression is refused as an unknown object. | The wildcard stands for the expression, which no single golden case can show; the function form of the fold needs symbolic output that the kernel does not have. |
| The work `Unique`, `Frequency` and `Remove` charge | `AlgoUnique` and `AlgoFrequency` put their values in a `TreeMap` (a lookup each), but a cumulative `Frequency` walks every bucket below each value again (`getCumFreq`) and so does every class of a class list, `AlgoRemove` scans the list to remove for every element, and the first appearance of `AlgoUnion.addToOutputList` scans the output built so far, with no bound. | The tables of `Unique` and `Frequency` are ordered maps like the `TreeMap` (`Unique(Sequence(500000))` is defined); the cumulative counts and the classes read the running sums of the table, a lookup each (`Frequency(true, Sequence(k, k, 1, 500000))` is defined), `Remove` charges the scan of every element — the product of both lengths — so `Remove(Sequence(500000), Sequence(500000))` is undefined past the work bound, and the first-appearance scan of the other kinds charges the same product. | The work bound (see the rows above) charges what the evaluation does; an unbounded scan would hang it instead. |
| Canvas lists and the Create List tool | Lists draw their anonymous members when drawable and can be picked through their members. The rectangle removes the selected polygon's sides and vertices before making a dependent list. | Draws and picks anonymous points, vectors, segments, lines, rays, circles and conics. Named members retain their own drawables. A bare number anywhere in a list suppresses it, including past its drawn prefix. Scans up to 256 entries per list, 512 per desktop frame and 4,096 per SVG drawing pass or pointer query. Anonymous circles and conics charge 64 units each. Create List handles enclosed points, segments, circles, polygons and polylines, up to 256 selected objects and 16 KiB of labels. Polygon filtering charges journal operations and member reads to a shared 4,096-unit allowance. | Other rectangle-selection shapes and individual member hit identities are omitted. |
| Canvas drawing limits | The reference draws all eligible regions and locus samples it has generated. | SVG draws up to eight Boolean regions across all layers. Desktop locus drawing and picking share the first 10,001 samples. Isolated samples are drawn and pickable in that prefix. | The caps bound graphics work. The SVG locus still draws the full stored locus. |
| Saved InputBox rounding | A per-box decimal-place or significant-figure override changes the displayed text and is written to the saved file. | Both overrides survive export and reload. The editor still uses its standard number and angle formatting for display. | Live per-box formatting is outside this round's export fix. |
| Saved sector fill | Zero opacity with no hatch leaves only the boundary pickable. A hatch enables interior picking. | Static saved opacity and hatch are used for the pick decision. The desktop renderer still uses its existing outline. | The saved pattern and inverse fill are retained for export but the desktop renderer does not paint them. |

## Geometry command arguments

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Point(path)` and `Point(path, number)` | Accept any `Path` implementation. | Accept lines, rays, segments, circles, general conics, polygons, polylines, arcs, sectors and Cartesian curves. | Path support follows the kernel's path parameter representations. |
| `Distance(point, path)` and `Distance(path, point)` | Use the path's closest point or its specialized distance formula. | Accept lines, rays, segments, conics, conic parts, polygons, polylines, functions and point lists. | The command uses the kernel's projection algorithms and the line and segment distance formulas. |
| `ClosestPoint(path, point)` and `ClosestPoint(point, path)` | Accept any `Path` implementation. | Accept lines, rays, segments, conics, conic parts, polygons, polylines, functions and point lists. | The command uses the kernel's projection algorithms. |
| `PointIn(region)` | Accepts any `Region` implementation. | Accepts polygons, circles, conics, arcs and sectors. The stored region parameters follow moves of these objects. | Region support follows the kernel's region coordinate representations. |
| `AreEqual(any, any)` | Uses object-specific equality, including symbolic comparison of function expressions. | Compares numbers and angles by their stored values and applies object equality to list entries. Numeric functions use the two preliminary sample checks, then compare polynomial coefficients through degree four. Other numeric functions compare by object identity. Boolean functions compare at inequality boundaries and between them when the kernel can extract the boundaries. | Symbolic equivalence requires a computer algebra system. |
| `AreCongruent(any, any)` | Uses each object's congruence rule. Some conic rules inspect the construction algorithm. | Compares lengths for segments, radii for circles and areas, sides and angles for polygons. A number or angle in the second argument compares against the polygon's area. Other conic types return false. | Definition-specific conic congruence is outside the numeric comparisons covered here. |
| `Length(conic)` | Measures arc length for a conic part. | `Length` accepts both arc and sector kinds. | Conic parts use separate kinds in the kernel. |
| `PolyLine(point, point, ..., boolean)` | A false flag makes a polyline. A true flag makes a smoothed pen stroke. | A false flag makes a polyline. A true flag returns an error. | Pen strokes require their own smoothing, representation and rendering. |
| `RigidPolygon(point, point, ...)` | Replaces the input points after the first with constrained points. | Reuses the input point identities when the first edge is nonzero and the free vertices permit the constraints in evaluation order. Other inputs use separate vertices constrained to translate with the anchor. | Installing the constraints when their inputs rank later would require changing the dependency order. |
| List-backed polygons and polylines | Use the current point list. | Use the current point list, including when its size changes. Indexed `Vertex` follows the current points. | An unindexed vertex command reserves its labelled outputs at creation, as for other variable-size polygon commands. |
| Drawing pad corners | The corner reads the selected view and, for index 6, the host window. | The corner reads the first kernel view supplied by the caller, or the headless default, and index 6 returns the headless window size of zero. | The kernel has a single graphics view and runs without a host window. |
| `Corner(inequality)` and `Vertex(inequality)` | Dispatches by border type and tests candidate vertices against the Boolean expression. A function border that is not polynomial meets only one-variable polynomial borders, at their zeros (`intParamYX`, `intParamXY`); its other pairs use Newton helpers that start from an undefined point and give no vertex. A border that is neither polynomial nor a function of one coordinate stops `FunctionNVar.initIneqs`, which leaves the rest of the tree empty, and testing a candidate then throws. Edits can add indexed outputs, and those labels remain when the vertices become undefined. | Supports the absolute-value rewrite and bounded polynomial border intersections. Linear pairs use homogeneous crossings, including the rounded crossing at `(2, 0)` in `corners-vertex-009`. Line-conic vertices follow the noncontinuous solver's order. Nonpolynomial function borders follow the reference pairs, including the doubled zero of `corners-vertex-013`. An incomplete tree rejects the command when a pair would test a candidate at creation (`corners-inequality-007` to `-011`); when that first happens after an edit, the vertices are undefined. The tree follows `FunctionNVar.initIneqs`: a negation is pushed into the comparisons, so a border that reads NaN stays false under it (`corners-review-002`, `-003`); `XOR`, a negated `XOR` and a negated comparison chain read as unknown (`corners-review-008`, `-009`, `-011`); a Boolean constant stops the tree (`corners-review-007`, `-012`). The pairs that read the zeros of a one-variable border (`intParamYX`, `intParamXY`, `intLinearX`, `intLinearY`, `intXY`) follow the reference in order and values, with the zeros found from the numerator of the border's normal form at its own coefficients (`corners-review-001`, `-006`, `-010`). A candidate is tested as `Inequality.valueAround` does: each border reads only the coordinates it has, and a border that reads NaN is false, so an undefined candidate such as `(-2, sqrt(-2))` drops out without hiding the other vertices (`corners-review-013` to `-015`). `AlgoVertexIneq` keeps one helper per border pair from the first update that needs it, of the class the border's polynomial form chose then: a polynomial helper later evaluates the live border on a vertical line and finds nothing on other lines, and a Newton helper never yields a vertex. So `corners-review-004` (polynomial helper from n = 2) finds `(1, 1)` at n = 0.5, `corners-review-016` (Newton helper from n = 0.5) finds nothing at n = 2, and `corners-review-017` finds `(4, 2)` at n = 0.5 from the current exponent. The kernel retains the chosen helper classes by command and border pair during live edits. Redefinitions of an input and restores through undo, redo or a saved construction rebuild the helpers. Edits add and retain indexed outputs. | The reference's exception after an edit is not recorded: the harness stops at it. Whether a pair tests a candidate follows the helpers of `AlgoVertexIneq.typeSwitch`, which test their undefined outputs too. |
| Inequality helper resets during snapshot replay | Loads the current construction once through `DefaultUndoManager.loadUndoInfo`. | Replays checkpoints to preserve intermediate edits. Each checkpoint refreshes slots evaluated since the previous checkpoint and propagates changed values. Legacy full-reset directives still share one million work units during text loading. Exhausting that allowance preserves the open construction. | Unrelated edits cannot schedule checkpoint work. The legacy allowance bounds repeated full resets in older scripts. |
| `Vertex(image, number)` | `AlgoImageCorner` uses raster anchors, raster dimensions and the active graphics view to compute world coordinates. A screen-fixed image yields an undefined point in Classic. | Supports raster image corners, including coincident anchors and screen-fixed images, in the kernel's graphics view. The Java archives `saved-vertex-image-001` through `003` cover initial, moved and degenerate states. | The kernel exposes one graphics view and uses the headless scales until the desktop supplies a sampling view. Early loading during archive import requires the image's anchor objects to have already been imported. |
| `Corner(text, number)`, `Vertex(text, number)` | Reads the drawable bounding box. The headless fixtures use `TextLayoutCommon` and `DrawEquationStub`. The desktop and web views measure platform fonts and formula glyphs. | Projects the headless text rectangle using the current view, the text anchor, the saved font size and three pixels of padding. | The kernel uses the same headless metric estimates on every platform. Its drawing canvas lays out the actual glyphs separately. Saved text anchors trigger updates when they move. If an anchor becomes undefined, GeoGebra keeps the last corner and the kernel makes it undefined (corners-textbounds-006). Retaining a value when its parent is undefined is pending the W1 decision. |
| `Vertex(boolean, number)` | `GeoButton.calculateFromBounds` reads the checkbox drawable's `labelRectangle`. For a labelled boolean that is not shown, `DrawBoolean.update` returns before `updateLabel`, so the rectangle stays the empty one at the pixel origin and corners 1 to 4 (the index cast with `(int)`) are `(xmin, ymax)` of the view; other indices give an undefined point, and so does an unlabelled boolean, which has no drawable. A shown checkbox measures its caption with the font. | Follows the hidden checkbox for every labelled boolean and recomputes on view changes. | A shown checkbox also gives the hidden corner because the displayed caption metrics depend on platform fonts, as for text. Recorded in `corners-checkbox-001` to `corners-checkbox-004`. |
| `IntersectPath(polyline, conic)`, `IntersectPath(conic, polyline)`, `IntersectPath(polygon, conic)`, `IntersectPath(conic, polygon)` with long paths | `AlgoIntersectPolyLineConicRegion` walks the input edges and sizes the output from the retained segments. | Charges for a linear pass over the whole path, including conic crossings and closure cuts. The 1,001-vertex paths in `georestclip-largepath-001` and `-002` clip within that allowance. | Each input pair shares its clip across output slots. The evaluation work and element limits bound oversized inputs and results. |
| `IntersectPath(polygon, line)` and the equivalent carrier forms | Splits the carrier at polygon edges and keeps intervals inside the polygon. | Charges for the vertices, actual cuts and containment scans. The 7,000-vertex polygon in `georestclip-longcarrier-001` has two cuts and clips within the budget. Outputs are undefined if the work allowance is exhausted. | Each distinct interval can require a containment scan. Polygons with many cuts can still reach the allowance. |
| `IntersectPath(polygon, polygon)`, `Difference(polygon, polygon)`, `Difference(polygon, polygon, boolean)` | `AlgoPolygonOperation` uses a polygon clipping library to return contours, their vertices and their edges. | The kernel uses its own overlay. Contour start points and orientation can differ, which also affects the order of labelled vertices and edges. For the diamond in `georestclip-overlaylabels-001`, GeoGebra labels (1, 0) as A and the kernel labels (0, 1) as A. Results are undefined when the overlay exceeds its work budget. | The kernel splits edges at crossings and traces contours with the result interior on the left. It chooses a contour start by vertex position. Pairwise edge tests, containment checks and boundary stitching are charged against the evaluation budget. Output label searches share a separate allowance and the command is refused if it runs out. |
| `Tangent(point, curve)` | `AlgoTangentCurve` uses the native parameter of a point on the same path or a point defined by `c(t)`. For other points it refines the closest parameter with the curve's extremum finder and takes the derivative there. | Uses the native path parameter for `Point(c)` and `Point(c, s)`, or the existing closest-parameter search for a point supplied independently of the curve. The contact point is the start of the tangent line. | Direct expressions of the form `c(t)` have yet to be lowered to point definitions. |
| `PenStroke(point, point, ...)` | `CmdPenStroke` reads point literals without resolving names, so a coordinate that reads an object is undefined and breaks the stroke. `GeoLocusStroke` smooths each subpath of three or more points into cubic pieces in the active view (pieces whose control points turn less than 3° stay straight, neighbours closer than 4 pixels are averaged, pieces longer than 50 pixels are subdivided once and smoothed again) and freezes the result. Saved files write every number with five significant digits. | Smooths the same way in the construction's view and freezes the stroke at creation; the journal keeps the frozen points, so a rebuild in another view restores them. The export writes every number exactly, which the reference reader parses, and the importer restores a reference file's rounded numbers as written. | The `stroke` group records smoothing, breaks, frozen inputs and four views; `saved-strokes-001` the rounded file. `stroke-limits-001` has 32,489 smoothed points in GeoGebra. The kernel marks it undefined at creation because its exact coordinates exceed the 1 MiB limit for a saved XML attribute and cannot round-trip through the importer. Older files with vertices (`strokeCoords`) are restored where no piece has three points; longer pieces are smoothed in whatever view the reference reader has, which the file does not determine, so they are skipped. A stroke whose points are all undefined is saved without points and reloads undefined, in the reference as well. Strokes are not yet paths or transformation inputs of other commands. |
| `PenStroke(number, number, ...)` | `CmdPenStroke` reads two consecutive number literals as a point. Signs fold only into numerals, `?`, `NaN` and `∞`; `e`, `pi`, `π` and `°` count unsigned. The second number of a pair is evaluated whatever it is, and a lone last number is refused. | Reads the same literals and pairs and passes them to the same stroke construction, with breaks between subpaths. | `stroke-literals-008` records the folded and refused forms; `stroke-numbers-004` mixes a point literal into the pairs. |

## Implicit polynomial equations

| Area | Upstream behavior | Kernel behavior | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Non-polynomial implicit equations | Accepts a two-variable residual such as `y^2 = sin(x)`. | Refuses non-polynomial input and makes a variable-degree equation undefined while it has no polynomial coefficients. | Implicit objects in this kernel require a finite coefficient matrix. |
| Implicit contour rendering | Can plot square-free factors of a residual separately and recognize isolated conic points. | Traces sign crossings on a bounded grid and tests local sign crossings for pointer hits. | Even-multiplicity branches and isolated zeros do not change sign. Features smaller than a grid cell can be missed. |
| Polynomial work budgets | May attempt arbitrarily large interpolation and elimination systems. | Limits the total degree of a polynomial equation to 32, point interpolation to degree 8, and the reduced Sylvester matrix to 12 rows. | The limits bound construction, numeric solvers and drawing on adversarial inputs. |

## Curve and implicit intersections

| Area | GeoGebra | Geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Saved labels for `Intersect(line, curve)` and `Intersect(curve, line)` | The construction can retain labelled slots after an edit reduces the number of roots. | Both argument orders preserve up to 512 saved slots. | The limit exceeds the number of roots supported by the kernel's polynomial and spline solvers and bounds allocation when loading output labels. |
| `Intersect(curve, curve)` with multiple solutions | The symbolic system solver orders parameter pairs before the command checks their domains. The recorded parabola returns positive first at integer-root heights and negative first at the tested irrational-root heights. | Bounded polynomial elimination orders real pairs by ascending first parameter and then ascending second parameter. Interval checks preserve those indexed slots, including undefined ones. | Numerical polynomial roots lack the symbolic ordering. This can exchange labels or move the only defined point to a different slot. The differences are recorded in the bounded, growth, independent, order, parabola and radical cases in `curvepairs`. |
| `Intersect(curve, curve)` with nonpolynomial coordinates | Solves the two parameter equations symbolically, including the square-root coordinate in `curvepairs-radical-001`. | The unseeded solver leaves the intersection undefined. The four-argument command uses the supplied Newton seeds. | Polynomial elimination requires polynomial coordinates in both parameters. |
| `Intersect(path, point)` and `Intersect(point, path)` | `AlgoIntersectPathPoint` projects onto any supported 2D path and compares the resulting coordinates with the original point. | Accepts line carriers, conics and conic parts, polygons, polylines, numeric function graphs, point lists and Cartesian curves. Loci and implicit curves are unsupported. | Incidence follows the kernel's path projection routines.
