Cartesian curve projection uses a bounded minimization and can depend on the approximation at a closest parameter. |
| Implicit intersection output budget | The graph and polyline algorithms size their output from the roots or crossings found. | Graphs and polylines charge work per result slot and use the general evaluation and element limits. Other implicit partners still have a 64-slot limit. | A result outside the applicable budget is undefined. Existing labelled slots can recover after an edit. |
| Implicit intersection polynomial degree budget | `AlgoIntersectImplicitPolynomials` builds a reduced Sylvester determinant and passes its coefficients to the numerical root solver. | Intersections and implicit tangent contacts share the reduced determinant (degree 1,024, 12 rows) and bounded deflation of high-degree projections. Graph and parametric substitution and the specialized conic projection still cap polynomial products at degree 64. | Two supported implicit equations have degree at most 32 each. The degree-72 projections in `implicit-pair-011` and `implicit-tangents-008` agree with the reference. |
| `Intersect(implicit, implicit)` near irrational tangencies | `implicit-pair-009` retains four approximate contacts even when the offset makes the exact curves disjoint. | Retains four points in the same order. Exact coordinate parity is pending for this case. | The repeated roots make the projected coordinates sensitive to root isolation and refinement. The resulting coordinates differ by more than the comparison tolerance. |

The 2D command dispatcher has no function–Cartesian curve overload.

## Algebraic geometry

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `LocusEquation(locus)`, `LocusEquation(point, point)` | Eliminates the driver coordinates from the traced construction (`locusEqu` in its CAS). The overall sign of the coefficients comes from the CAS's elimination of a system whose polynomial and variable lists are in hash-set order. | Rounds free coordinates to the prover precision and keeps the constraints of constructed points. The circle, parabola, midpoint, and three radius forms of the conchoid agree, as do ellipses and hyperbolas from a perpendicular bisector, feet of perpendiculars and midpoints driven along a line, a segment or a circle. The term with the largest power of x (then of y) has a positive coefficient. | `locusequation-classic-001`, `-002`, `-005` and `-006` agree up to the overall sign (see the sign row of `Envelope`). The locus-equation translator has no exclusion for an indexed intersection that shares the mover (`locusequation-classic-008`), so its projection is undefined. Inline arguments and symbolic point constructions beyond the supported midpoint and intersection rules remain pending. |
| `LocusEquation(boolean, point)` | Projects an algebraic condition on a movable point. Translating the condition rewrites the Boolean for good (`DependentBooleanAdapter.getBotanaPolynomials`): each distance operand of its root becomes an existing segment between the same points, or a new segment whose length is never computed and reads 0. Later updates of the Boolean compare those segments. | Translates `==` of two point-to-point distances or segments (equal squared lengths, `AlgoAreCongruent`) and makes the same rewrite when the command is created. The reference makes it only once the construction has been translated; the kernel makes it always. A distance compared with a number and the other comparisons are undefined, as recorded. | `locusequation-boolean-001` to `-013` agree, including the existing segment (`-009`), inline distances (`-010`), a number (`-011`) and `<` (`-013`). |
| Locus polynomial budgets | May use more variables and project more general elimination ideals. | Uses exact rational coefficients, up to eight auxiliary variables, a degree limit of 16 and a 500,000-unit work budget. Finite real solutions of a multi-generator plane ideal are represented by a product of zero-radius circles. | Systems beyond those limits or with non-principal positive-dimensional plane ideals yield undefined. |
| `Envelope(path, point)` | Forms the algebraic condition for contact with the moving family and eliminates the mover (`envelopeEqu`). The sign of the coefficients comes from its CAS, as for `LocusEquation`. | Projects the carrier, the family, and their tangency condition. A single-point family member supplies no circle-incidence equation. Free numeric radii stay symbolic and the recorded envelopes have the zero equation. Perpendicular bisectors and perpendicular lines driven along a line or a circle, and circles through a fixed point, agree. The sign follows the kernel's normalization of `LocusEquation`. | `envelope-classic-001` step 2 agrees up to the overall sign: the recorded inputs of `locusequation-midpoint-001` step 2 and `locusequation-classic-005` both use the curve variables v9 and v10, yet every coefficient sign is opposite, so no fixed variable order decides it. Other path families and constructions whose auxiliary point constraints require a larger contact Jacobian remain pending. |
| `Prove(boolean)`, `ProveDetails(boolean)` | The desktop application tries a numerical method, then the algebraic method (Gröbner bases in the CAS), then an external Wu prover. The result depends on the construction alone and is recomputed only when the statement's text changes. | Runs the algebraic method on its own exact arithmetic: integer polynomials, a fraction-free Buchberger algorithm with the Gebauer and Möller criteria, and an elimination order. Fixes the first two free points at (0, 0) and (0, 1), or only the first one when a point lies on a line or a circle has a numerical radius. A zero elimination ideal with a nonzero ideal for the thesis is false. When both are zero, the Hilbert dimension of the hypotheses decides between "true on components" and unknown. Translates the predicate statements `AreCollinear`, `AreParallel`, `ArePerpendicular`, `AreConcurrent`, `AreConcyclic` and `AreEqual` of points, lines or circles. The supported objects are free points, lines, segments, midpoints, intersections of lines, perpendicular and parallel lines, perpendicular bisectors, circles through a point or three points or with a free numeric radius, points on lines and circles, circle intersections, and intersections of a line, segment or ray with a focus-directrix parabola. Reads conditions by dividing the generator by the irreducible primitive candidates in the reference's lookup order. An axis among the predecessors gives the reference's unknown result. Everything else, or more than 30,000,000 work units (term operations weighted by coefficient size), 40 variables, 600 basis elements or 64-limb coefficients, is undefined. | The Java harness runs the reference's algebraic engine with native Giac. The numerical and external engines are not on its class path. A statement written as an expression is translated as the reference does: `==` of two points, lines, segments, circles or point-to-point distances is `AlgoAreCongruent` (segments and distances by length, circles by radius), a distance operand is replaced by a segment as in the `LocusEquation(boolean, point)` row (also in the Boolean itself), a distance among the predecessors declares the first coordinate of its first point free, and a formula over segments with integer coefficients, `+`, `-`, `*` and integer powers is rewritten by eliminating the segment variables s from the formula and s^2 = v^2 with the kernel's own basis, fixing only the first point. An elimination with more than one element is undefined (the reference takes the first element of its CAS's list). The elimination squares each length, so it is blind to the sign of a term; the reference is too and proves `s + t == 0` and `s == -t` for equal segments (`prove-expression-019`, `-021`). A distance operand of the root of a formula, such as `Distance(A, B) == 2s`, is unknown in the reference, also for a second proof and after a redefinition, which rewrites the Boolean again (`prove-expression-017`). `==` of a ray, a line or a segment with another one of these, except two segments, is collinearity of their points (`prove-expression-024` to `-027`). An existing segment made after the Boolean replaces its distance and is read before it is updated, in the order of the construction, as in the reference (`prove-expression-018`, whose values do not tell the two orders apart). Other comparisons are unknown, as in the reference. `ProveDetails` of a formula is undefined: the reference words those conditions from its CAS's factorization (`prove-expression-012`, `-014`, `-019` to `-021`). A labeled distance inside a formula, such as `2d == r`, makes the reference throw a `ClassCastException` and could not be recorded; the kernel answers undefined. `ProveDetails` is undefined when the elimination basis has several elements that share variables. In that case the reference's choice depends on its CAS's term order. A condition on lines, which the reference names by looking up or creating lines, is also undefined. For an indexed intersection using a hidden intersection algorithm, both systems exclude an existing common point by adding a nonzero squared-distance constraint. The circle-pair algorithm does this when there is exactly one common point. Each output caches the constraints of its first translation. An unindexed command or saved output slot keeps the common point, as does an indexed output first translated after the intersection is labelled. A redefinition that rebuilds the construction clears the intersection translations. Childless edits keep them. The construction caches up to 4,096 translated intersection outputs, at most 8,192 entries across its two maps. A proof that needs another entry returns undefined until a rebuild frees the cache. When recomputing a proof during the edit, only slots that precede the proof in symbolic replay order suppress the common-point exclusion. Proofs outside the edited object's dependency graph keep their displayed answers but do not retranslate their intersections during replay (`prove-replay-003`). Segments and rays use their supporting line for these conic intersections. Parabola proofs use the focus, the perpendicular foot on the directrix, and equality of squared distances. A point, free number, or arithmetic number selector uses the same equations as a literal index. A conditional number selector makes the proof unknown. A translated intersection keeps its equations through a drag that makes the parabola a double line. Ellipses and hyperbolas from foci remain unsupported (`prove-focalconic-001` and `-002`). The reference's focus-and-point adapter uses four auxiliary distances, four squared-distance equations, and one equation relating the sums or differences of those distances. When the hidden intersection already has a translated output and a later translation also adds a common-point exclusion, the reference adapters overwrite one equation and leave a null entry, so the synchronous harness raises an exception. The desktop application and the kernel return an undefined Boolean for `Prove` and a defined empty list for `ProveDetails`. If a common point required by the exclusion is outside the statement's predecessors, the kernel answers undefined. |
| `ProveDetails(boolean, boolean)` | The relation tool form words recognized geometric conditions as localized sentences and falls back to symbolic text for other conditions, including formulas over quantities. | Returns an undefined list when the proof produces readable conditions. Other outcomes use the same list as `ProveDetails(boolean)`. | The localized sentences use the reference's language files (`prove-relation-001`, `-002`). The symbolic equation `s = t` in `prove-expression-019` comes from `NDGDetector`'s formula parser. |
| Distinctness of indexed and labelled circle intersections | When the helper has several translated outputs, the reference's peer choice depends on identity-hash iteration order. | Uses the last translated candidate in the order of the construction's child IDs. | For `prove-peer-001`, the reference returned false with `{false}` in one recording and an undefined Boolean with `{}` in the other two. The kernel reports true on components. |
| Prover recordings | The desktop application stops the prover after five seconds (`ProverD`) and pins Giac 70501. | Nothing to stop: the kernel's own arithmetic has a work allowance instead. | The harness decides synchronously without a deadline and loads the published Giac 70507 library. The longest proof in the latest recording took 441 ms. A proof the desktop would abandon after five seconds would still be recorded as decided. |

## Calculus commands

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The variable forms `Derivative(function, any)`, `Derivative(curve, any)`, `Derivative(function, any, number)`, `Derivative(curve, any, number)`, `Integral(function, number)`, `IntegralBetween(function, number)` and `NIntegral(function, number)` | The second argument is the function's variable under any name; the command processor registers it as a local variable (`resArgsLocalNumVar`). The two-argument form checks the name against the variable (`Derivative(c, t)` names the curve's parameter; `Derivative(f, g)` of two functions and `Derivative(c, x)` with the wrong name are argument errors), while the three-argument forms accept any name or label and ignore it. | A function is always in `x`: the two-argument variable form takes the literal `x` for a function and the curve's own parameter name for a curve, anything else is refused. The three-argument forms accept any identifier or label as the ignored variable, exactly as the reference, with the third argument the order. `Integral` keeps the `x` spelling. | The kernel has one function variable. The golden cases record the `x` and parameter spellings. |
| A conditional function whose branch conditions are not plain comparisons of `x` with a number (`Integral`, `NIntegral`, `IntegralBetween`; `Bounds.addRestriction`) | Substitutes a called boolean function into the condition (`unfunction`), so `If(b(x), 1, 2)` with `b(x) = x > 2` still gives the branch interval; an `OR` condition that stays valid on both sides drops the next restriction; a nested `If` written as the trailing else of a multi-condition `If` is kept as one case function. | The call, the `OR` conjunction and the nested multi-condition `If` stay residual conditions (or fold into the pair list), so the interval split gives up and the whole function runs through the plain adaptive quadrature, which is GeoGebra's own fallback for a condition the bounds cannot express. The plain comparisons, `&&`, chains such as `1 < x < 3`, `==` and `!=` agree, as the golden cases record. | The residual conditions of `Bounds` are conjunction trees over GeoGebra's own variable objects; the kernel's conditions are the source tree itself. |
| `Polynomial(point, point, ...)` through very ill-conditioned points | `polcoeBig` runs the interpolation recurrence on `BigDecimal`s. | The same recurrence on `f64`. Well-conditioned points agree within the golden tolerance. | No decimal arithmetic in the kernel; the golden cases stay inside it. |
| `NDerivative(function)` of `x!` or `gamma(x)` | The rule tree is `psi(x + 1) x!` (an `Operation.PSI` node). | The kernel has no psi operation, so the rule leaves `NaN` and the derivative is undefined. | Psi is not among the kernel's operations; no golden case exercises it. |
| `Derivative` of anything that is no polynomial | With the CAS (a full GeoGebra) the symbolic derivative answers: `Derivative(sin(x))` is `cos(x)`, and a high power of a non-polynomial base (`sin(x)^11`) too. | Only the polynomial path exists, exactly as GeoGebra without a CAS: the derivative is undefined for every non-polynomial (`Function.getDerivative` reaches its CAS branch), while `NDerivative` keeps GeoGebra's rule tree and answers. | The polynomial CAS does not perform symbolic differentiation. The headless harness records the no-CAS answers for the derivative cases. |
| `Integral(function)` of anything that is no polynomial, and the definite `Integral`, `IntegralBetween` and `NIntegral` with an infinite bound or an endpoint where the function is undefined or infinite | With the CAS the antiderivative answers symbolically, and `computeSpecial` integrates improper integrals, infinite bounds included. | The antiderivative exists for polynomials only (plus the rules of `NIntegral`); the definite integral of a non-polynomial is the adaptive quadrature over finite bounds where the endpoints are defined, and undefined otherwise, as in GeoGebra without a CAS. | Same reason. The adaptive value agrees with GeoGebra's numerical integration in the golden cases. |
| `SolveODE(function)` and `SolveODE(function, point)` | Both symbolic forms call the CAS, including for polynomial slopes. Without symbolic evaluation both return undefined. With it, the form without a point can expose an adjustable integration constant. | Solves univariate polynomial slopes in x by integration, using a zero constant or choosing the constant through the point. Curves give undefined functions in both implementations. | General symbolic integration, adjustable integration constants and an ODE solver beyond polynomial slopes are absent. The `odesymbolic` cases record native results and the undefined results without symbolic evaluation. |
| `SolveODE` with symbolic evaluation disabled (`odesymbolic-nocas-001`) | Both symbolic forms are undefined, including for polynomial slopes. | Integrates supported polynomial slopes directly and gives both functions when the slope is defined. | The kernel has no setting that disables its polynomial integrator. |
| `Degree((a x + b)^300)` (`calculus-degree-010`) | Expanding the power exhausts the heap. The CAS fallback reports degree 0 when `a = 0` and the other degree snapshots are undefined. The old recording also stores overflowing function samples as null. | Shares coefficient subtrees and returns a degree. The overflowing function samples are positive infinity, as recorded without `Degree` in `infinity-outputs-031`. | The kernel can compute the result within its memory and work budgets. |
| `Derivative` and `NDerivative` of curves and two-variable functions | Differentiates both curve coordinates and keeps the parameter interval. It can also differentiate a function of two variables partially. | Differentiates coordinate expressions for Cartesian curves, splines, conditional curves, derivatives and transformed curves. The golden cases verify dilations, affine and projective matrices, and parametric derivatives. Partial differentiation of a two-variable function is unavailable. | Curve differentiation can use one parameter in both coordinate expressions. A partial derivative requires binding the selected variable in a two-variable expression. |
| Work of the calculus commands | Unlimited: a polynomial expansion, a high rule-derivative order or a huge coefficient evaluation runs as long as it takes. | Inside the kernel's expression work budget: each power exponent and each polynomial coefficient is charged when it is finished and evaluated, one rule-derivative pass is charged its tree and may not build past a node cap (4 000 000 nodes), and past the budget the result is undefined. A polynomial power is bounded on its merged terms (4096, a bound GeoGebra does not have) and one multiplication on a memory bound of raw unmerged terms, so `(x + 1)^300` and `(a x + b)^300` answer as they should while dense multivariate products past the memory bound stay undefined in bounded time. `NDerivative(x sin(x) cos(x), 25)` is undefined after about half the budget, where GeoGebra would build tens of millions of nodes. | A stray large input must not stall the application or exhaust memory on every update. Orders GeoGebra computes within a comparable effort (up to about the 12th of that product) still answer. |
| `Spline(list)`, `Spline(list, number)` and their derivatives on long or ill-conditioned systems | Solves the spline system with its own Gaussian elimination: rows sorted by a selection sort at every column, the pivot row rescaled by each multiplier. Its matrix entries use `Math.pow`, its point values `Math.sin` and the other elementary functions of the JVM. | Performs the same row operations on sparse rows, so the coefficients are bit-identical when the matrix entries and point values are, and the work follows the band of the system (a 260-point spline solves well inside the budget, a degree-300 spline of 300 points is refused by the entry bound). Integer powers are correctly rounded and `sin` follows the portable fdlibm algorithm. | The recording JVM's `Math.pow` intrinsic is off by one ulp when the exact power lies within about 0.003 ulp of a rounding midpoint, and its `Math.sin` differs by one ulp from fdlibm for some arguments. A spline with hundreds of pieces in the monomial basis amplifies such an ulp past 1e-9. Cases `funcrest-derivative-004` (seven `sin(k)` inputs) and `funcrest-splinelong-003` (24 powers) record it; `funcrest-splinelong-001`, `-002` and `-005` to `-008` agree. |
| `Spline(list)` and `Spline(list, number)` after the list changes size | `AlgoSpline.compute` checks the degree against the size of its point array, which it resizes only after the check passed: the number of points of the last accepted solve, or of the list at creation. A spline whose list shrank below the degree and grew again stays undefined; one created with too few points never becomes defined. When the stale size admits a degree above the number of points and the system has extra degree rows, the reference throws an index exception. | The same check against the same remembered size, kept per spline object across updates and dropped on rollback or redefinition. A locus samples a copy of the construction whose splines start fresh and keep their own sizes between updates, as the reference's copy does; the live sizes are untouched by sampling. The end conditions use the factorials as 32-bit integers, wrapping from 13! on, converted through `float`. Where the reference throws, the spline is undefined. | Cases `funcrest-splinelong-005` to `-010` record shrinking and growing lists, a locus over a shrinking list and the wrapped factorials; the exception cannot be recorded by the harness. |
| A slider locus whose copy's spline fails its degree check | The locus samples a copy made when the locus is created; a point on the copy's spline keeps its last position while the spline is undefined, so the samples repeat that point (a scratch reference run of `a = Slider(2, 4)`, `s = Spline(Sequence((k, k), k, 1, 6 - a))` moved to 4, `P = Point(s, 0.5)`, `Q = Midpoint(P, (0, 0))`, `Locus(Q, a)` gives 129 samples at (0.75, 0.75)). | The copy is made at the same moment and its splines start from the list as it is then, but a point on an undefined path is undefined, so such samples are left out and this locus is undefined. | How the reference's path point keeps its position on an undefined path is not settled by the readable sources (the same open question as the point optimizers on an undefined path), so the kernel does not guess it. The harness cannot move the slider between script lines, so no golden case records it. |
| Expanding a power with object coefficients, like `(a x + b)^300` | The structural expansion exhausts the reference harness heap. In `calculus-degree-010`, the native CAS fallback returns degree zero when `a = 0` and the other three snapshots remain undefined. | The coefficient trees share subtrees in one arena. Evaluating each shared subtree once gives the degree in every snapshot. | The kernel bounds its work and memory on each update. |
| The coefficients of the function `Polynomial(f)` and the polynomial `Derivative(f)` build | The expanded function keeps the coefficient trees, symbolic in the objects they read. | The terms keep the evaluated numbers of the coefficients, recomputed whenever the source function's objects change (the definition's parents keep the dependency). | A result tree that kept the shared coefficient subtrees would evaluate each of them once per reference on every evaluation, unbounded work for a power like `(a x + b)^300`. Only the symbolic form of the definition differs; the values agree, as the golden cases record. |

## Text objects and commands

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Text(any)`, `Text(any, boolean)`, `Text(any, point)`, `Text(any, point, boolean)`, `Text(any, point, boolean, boolean)`, `Text(any, point, boolean, boolean, number)`, `Text(any, point, boolean, boolean, number, number)` | Displays the formatted value or definition of any object. | Supports every argument signature. Values and symbolic definitions use the formatting available for the kernel's object kinds and expression nodes. | Some object classes and symbolic representations require features outside the current kernel. |
| `LaTeX(any)`, `LaTeX(any, boolean)`, `LaTeX(any, boolean, boolean)` | Formats the value or definition using its LaTeX template, with an optional object name. | Supports all three signatures and the `FormulaText` spelling with the kernel's supported objects and symbolic operations. | CAS objects and some object-specific notation require additional formatting support. |
| Text rendering | The graphics and algebra views use TeX math fonts and layout metrics. | The canvas, algebra panel and SVG share box layout for the saved-text subset. The algebra panel typesets LaTeX text, function definitions and numeric matrices. See [the syntax inventory](docs/latex.md). | Bundled sans and monospace faces, approximate kerning, synthesized bold and italic, and scaled delimiter glyphs give different metrics. The saved serif choice and several Greek variant glyphs use the closest bundled face or character. |
| Unsupported LaTeX | The reference accepts additional macros and environments. | Unsupported commands and their arguments appear in magenta. Malformed groups and environments show their source in the same color. | Examples include macro definitions and packages (`\newcommand`, `\def`, `\usepackage`), editor placeholders (`\jlminput`, `\jlmcursor`), specialized alphabets (`\mathbb`, `\mathcal`, `\mathfrak`, `\Re`, `\Im`), custom equation tags, arbitrary array column expansion, and graphics packages such as TikZ. Automatic equation numbering is omitted and very large input is bounded. |
| Unicode text storage | Java strings can retain isolated UTF-16 surrogate halves. | Unicode commands count and index UTF-16 units. A result containing an isolated surrogate uses `?` for that unit, as the headless harness does when writing UTF-8. | Kernel text is UTF-8. |
| `SurdText(number, list)` | Uses CAS to format non-integer relations with the supplied constants. | Reproduces the headless harness output. A recognized non-integer relation that requires CAS produces `?`. | Symbolic CAS formatting is unavailable in the kernel. Bounded relation fitting can choose a different candidate from CAS. |
| `SurdText(point)` with complex or 3D coordinates | `AlgoSurdTextPoint` formats complex points in real-plus-imaginary notation and can include a third coordinate for 3D points. | Formats a Cartesian 2D coordinate pair. | Kernel points have two Cartesian coordinates and carry no complex or 3D coordinate mode. |
| TableText cell styling | Uses the input objects' current text and color styles. | Formats the basic table layouts and the default colors of supported labelled cells. | Arbitrary object coloring and table cell formatting require a style model for the source objects. |
| Exact arithmetic display in algebra templates | Symbolic mode shows fractions and reduces square roots. Other results follow the document rounding settings. | The pair runner compares ordinary numeric values using its implemented templates. | The product has no matching symbolic algebra value template for these results. Those pairs retain their formatting gaps. |

## Remaining differences in verified kernel tests

| Area | Reference | Kernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Axis steps after zooming and observations after `SetValue` or `SetCoords` | The scripting steps mutate the construction or the view and later reads observe the new state. | The scripting calls are refused by the kernel command entry point. The construction and view keep their previous values. | These steps exercise app scripting. Points with infinite coordinates also exceed the finite geometry representation. |
| `SlowPlot` result count | The scripting command creates a function and animation state and returns zero results to the input helper. | The labelled construction command returns its function. | The construction command exposes the object it creates. |
| `CurvatureVector(point, function)`, polynomial `Factors`, and rational-function `CommonDenominator` | The recorded tests use an empty symbolic backend. The sine vector and factor list are undefined, and the common denominator function has no value. | The numeric curvature and exact polynomial operations produce defined results. | The empty backend cannot compute the intermediate functions or polynomials. The sine vector difference also appears in a recorded golden case. |
| `Frequency(list, list)` with two text lists | Produces a contingency table. | Two list arguments are interpreted as numeric class boundaries and data. | Text contingency tables need a separate form. |
| Inline `ParametricDerivative(Curve(...))` | Constructs the inner curve, then uses its coordinate derivatives. | The construction now evaluates the inline curve. The verified step `commands/CommandsTest.java:3167` still has no typed output to compare. | The recording contains only the curve's printed string. |
| Inline slope and point in `Locus(x + y, Point(...))` | Resolves both expressions and returns a locus with the command definition as its text. | The inline slope resolver expects a named point in this form. | The older verified pair contains only the locus definition. The named inputs work through the existing locus form. |
| Parametric equations, complex function arguments, and 3D reflection | Accepts unbound curve parameters, an imaginary unit as a function input, and 3D lines and surfaces. | Supports explicit 2D curve definitions and real function arguments. | These inputs need separate parameter binding, complex evaluation, or 3D geometry. |
| Numeric solver and plot output | Uses its own curvature differences, extremum search, root isolation, rate solver, arc integration, and normal quantiles. | Computes these with the kernel's numeric routines. | Some final digits differ in the full-precision strings. The bounded sine extremum pair also differs in its recorded point coordinate beyond the golden tolerance. |
| XML number and function text | The XML template prints function bodies, rational coefficients, conditional expressions and exact numbers. | The pair runner formats the supported scalars and graph data. Function, curve, equation and inline polynomial text are missing in this template. | Display gaps remain where the product provides no equivalent text. Numeric rounding and solver precision can also change the exact printed digits. |
| Repeated `Curvature` and `CurvatureVector` outputs | Some test steps reuse an existing label for the new output. | The runner submits the recorded label. The kernel refuses a replacement of this definition kind. | The pair values require replacement support for these output definitions. |
| `IntersectPath(polygon, line)` output label | The unassigned test returns a segment under its default label. | The explicitly named form can generate an indexed label. | The label mismatch is checked directly in the pair replay. |
| Incidence lookup for `Intersect(c, c, n)` | A circle with itself can return an existing intersection point incident to the circle. | A circle with no defining point returns undefined when it has more than 250,000 direct dependents to search. | The incidence search has an explicit work limit. A partial scan can select the wrong point after redefinition changes evaluation order. |
| Nonfinite samples in recordings before schema 4 | These files write `null` for NaN and for either infinity. | A schema 4 recording, or the recording paired with the case in `tools/ggb-harness/cases/legacy-infinity.txt`, supplies the sign of each infinity. Any other `null` sample requires NaN or no sample. | `golden/calculus/degree.json` cannot be recorded again because the reference runs out of heap in `calculus-degree-010`. That case keeps a known gap at `1:f`. `infinity-outputs-031` records the same function with positive infinity at both ends. |
| Matrix images of curves and functions | `GeoCurveCartesian.matrixTransform` builds each image coordinate as an expression, and `ExpressionNode.multiply` and `plus` drop a term with a zero entry and leave out a zero addend. A NaN or infinite coordinate reaches only the coordinates whose entries read it, and signed zeros follow the expression. | Plain arithmetic, as on main: 0 * NaN and 0 * infinity are NaN, and zero terms are added. | Reproducing the simplification needs the expression form of every curve source, including functions, derivatives and dilations. Cases `georestsmall-matrixfunction-002`, `georestsmall-matrixcurve-005` and `georestsmall-matrixcurve-006` record the difference. |

## Charts

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `BarChart(number, number, any, number, number, number)` | Evaluates the expression over the local variable's range and draws one bar per result. | Lowers the range to the existing budgeted `Sequence` implementation. | The command uses the same sequence mechanism for its local variable and range. |
| `BarChart(number, number, any, number, number, number, number)` | Also accepts an explicit sequence step. | The step is passed to the budgeted `Sequence` implementation. | The step and range follow the same sequence rules as the six-argument form. |
| `Histogram(boolean, list, list, boolean, number, any)` | Uses the first five arguments. | Uses the first five arguments. | The sixth argument is ignored by the command processor. |
| `HistogramRight(boolean, list, list, boolean, number, any)` | Uses the first five arguments. | Uses the first five arguments. | The sixth argument is ignored by the command processor. |
| Stacked dots | Drawing the density plot updates the coordinates of the output list. | The list uses the harness view at 50 pixels per unit. The window adapts the stacked positions to the current camera. | Following the camera in the drawing keeps construction evaluation independent of the viewport. |

## Discrete geometry

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Convex hull implementation (`Hull`, `ConvexHull`) | `AlgoConvexHull` delegates the hull calculation to a bundled library. | Independent gift wrapping from [Jarvis (1973)](https://doi.org/10.1016/0020-0190(73)90020-3). The kernel groups near-duplicates using sorted coordinate bands and charges the grouping and wrapping to the evaluation budget. Cancellation-sensitive turns use directed predecessor links to reproduce the reference's near-collinear rounding. | Only the Java harness output was used to match the starting vertex, clockwise order, duplicate representatives and closure. See `discrete-hull-*` and `misc-hull-*`. |
| Minimum spanning tree edge order and ties | Rebuilding the graph can change edge order and which equal-weight edge enters the tree. In `discrete-tree-003`, returning to the original square can give a different tree. | Independent dense Prim using input-pair order for equal weights and emitted edges. | `PrimMinimumSpanningTree` and `MinimumSpanningForest2` have EUPL headers. The multigraph and utility classes lack them. `HashSet<TreeLink>` uses identity hashes because `TreeLink` has no `hashCode` override, and `UndirectedSparseMultigraph` is hash based. Their iteration order can vary across rebuilds and runs. |
| Equal-cost shortest paths | The chosen path can change after a recomputation even when two routes have the same cost. See `discrete-path-004`. | Independent Dijkstra using first occurrence of each endpoint to settle equal distances. For segment definitions that supply only coordinates, identical endpoint coordinates identify a vertex. | The hash-based `SparseMultigraph` uses identity hashes for its edge objects. Its iteration order can vary across rebuilds and runs, so equal-cost route selection has no reproducible order. The multigraph and utility classes lack EUPL headers. |
| `MinimumSpanningTree(number, number, ...)` | The shared dispatcher can wrap numbers into a list, but the algorithm rejects a list of multiple numbers when it reads them as points. | Reports a command error. | A number supplies no point coordinates. The harness records the rejection in `discrete-tree-001`. |
| Traveling salesman with an undefined site on update | The tour heuristic can throw while reading the missing site. | The locus is undefined until the sites are defined again. | A partial tour would be misleading. Both reject a missing site at creation, as recorded in `discrete-tour-009`. |
| Discrete graph work limit | Work depends on list size, geometry and the tour's improvement steps. | A graph is undefined when its computation exhausts the kernel's work budget. | Moving a site or growing a list recomputes the graph and must finish within a bounded amount of work. |

## Polynomial CAS

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Expand(function)`, `Simplify(function)`, `Simplify(number)`, `Factor(function)` | Giac chooses the form of the expansion, cancellation and factorization. | Stores exact rational coefficients. Expansion uses descending lexicographic monomials. Simplification cancels a polynomial GCD and uses a monic denominator. Factor displays primitive factors and a separate scalar. | Factor order, signs, rational presentation and the representation of a cancelled pole can differ. Golden snapshots compare sampled values and Rust tests check exact reconstruction. |
| `IFactor(function)` | Factors over extensions of the rationals, including irrational factors. | Factors over the rationals and displays additional exact factors for real quadratics and negative-constant pure powers of degree a power of two. | Splitting other factors over algebraic extensions needs a general algebraic-number field. The remaining factors keep their exact rational form. |
| CAS-view rows requiring Giac | Evaluates symbolic and numerical commands, including `NSolutions` with a starting value. | The default build evaluates a bounded subset of CAS rows and reports that commands such as `Numeric` require a CAS build. | The default build has no native Giac backend. The feature profile evaluates these rows with Giac. |
| Failed CAS calls with the `giac` feature (docs/cas-giac.md, "Backends and failures") | A failed Giac call (time limit, error) leaves the CAS command's result undefined; its CAS backend is always present. | With the feature the backend is chosen once per process. Without a configured library the kernel behaves exactly like the default build, refusing the forms only Giac evaluates. With the library, a failed Giac call makes the result undefined and never falls back to the kernel's solver. A configured library that fails the hash check or does not load makes every such result undefined and is reported once. | The reference cannot run without its CAS, the kernel can: a build with the feature but no library keeps the default build's answers instead of losing them, and a configured backend never silently swaps for another. |
| `Solve(function, any)`, `Solutions(function, any)`, `NSolve(function, any)`, `NSolutions(function, any)`, `NSolve(line, any)`, `NSolutions(line, any)`, `NSolve(conic, any)`, `NSolutions(conic, any)`, `NSolutions(implicit, any)`, `NSolutions(list, any)`, `NSolve(implicit, any)`, `NSolve(list, any)`, `Solutions(line, any)`, `Solutions(conic, any)`, `Solutions(implicit, any)`, `Solutions(list, any)`, `Solve(line, any)`, `Solve(conic, any)`, `Solve(implicit, any)`, `Solve(list, any)` with the `giac` feature | `AlgoSolve` hands the equation and the second argument (solving variables, a start value `variable = value`, a number or another name) to Giac. | With the `giac` feature the kernel builds the same Giac input and evaluates it with the same Giac build (docs/cas-giac.md); every recorded case of these forms agrees exactly. Without the feature the kernel's own solver answers as before. | The second argument may be any expression. The recorded cases cover variables, lists of variables, start values, numbers, other objects and unknown names; the kernel prints any other second argument from its text with the reference's template rules, which no case checks. |
| `CompleteSquare(function)`, `IsFactored(function)`, `IsFactored(number)` | Checks and rewrites the structure of the input. | Completes a square for a quadratic in a single positive integer power of x. Checks the input factors of univariate polynomials through degree 5. | The displayed square and factored expressions can differ in order and signs. Factored-form recognition is limited to rational polynomial syntax. |
| `Solve(list, any)` | Accepts a variable binding or other CAS argument and can return parameterized assignment expressions. | Accepts a variable name or list of up to three distinct names with a bounded polynomial system. Renders linear assignments in two dimensions. | The result representation is limited to numeric values, 2D lines and affine functions of one parameter. |
| `Solutions(list, any)` | Can return general parameterized solutions to polynomial systems. | Accepts a variable name or list of up to three distinct names. Returns isolated real tuples, one-parameter affine families, and unions of affine families from a product equation. | Nonlinear positive-dimensional families and multiple free parameters require a general symbolic parameter representation. |
| `NSolve(list, any)` | Supports optional numeric guesses and wider classes of equations. | Accepts a variable name or list of up to three distinct names and finds the real roots of bounded polynomial systems. | Roots are isolated without an initial guess. Numeric initial guesses and transcendental equations remain outside this implementation. |
| `NSolutions(list, any)` | Supports optional numeric guesses and wider classes of equations. | Accepts a variable name or list of up to three distinct names and returns the real values of bounded polynomial systems. | Roots are isolated without an initial guess. Numeric initial guesses and transcendental equations remain outside this implementation. |
| Solve result form | Giac orders the solutions and chooses exact formulas or numerical roots. The order of a system's solutions follows its internal solving path and has no rule across inputs (`Solutions({x^2=1},{x})` answers `{1},{-1}` while `Solutions({x^4-16=0},{x})` answers `{-2},{2}`). | Real roots are kept as exact rationals and supported radicals where available. Other roots are isolated and refined in double-double arithmetic from the exact coefficients, so even the Wilkinson products answer every root to the last bit of a double. The kernel always answers single-equation roots and system rows in ascending order of the first coordinate. The golden runner records GeoGebra's actual order, and `Element` cases lock it. | Expression syntax, order and rounding of displayed roots can differ; where GeoGebra's system order differs from the kernel's fixed one the multisets still agree (recorded as known gaps). The golden tolerance remains 1e-9. Complex solution commands are outside this lane. |
| `Expand(list)`, `Expand(line)`, `Expand(conic)`, `Factor(list)`, `Factor(line)`, `Factor(conic)`, `IFactor(list)`, `IFactor(line)`, `IFactor(conic)` | `CmdCASCommand1Arg` takes an `AlgebraicExpression` only: a function, a curve or an implicit curve. A list, a line (also a segment) and a conic are an illegal argument. | Refused with an error, for named objects and inline lists or equations alike. | The same refusal as the reference, recorded by the `reject` steps of `cas-rejected-001` to `cas-rejected-003`. |
| `Simplify(text)` | `AlgoSimplifyText` tidies the signs of a formula written as a text with a fixed list of replacements (`+-` to `-`, `+1x` to `+x`, a space on either side of `+`, `-` and `=`, the minus sign after a space). An empty text throws when the command is created. | The same replacements in the same order, each pass from left to right without overlap. An empty or undefined text is refused when the command is created and gives an undefined text when it becomes empty later. | The reference fails on the first character of an empty text; a later update there has no defined result to reproduce. `golden/cas/text.json` records the texts, among them the upstream test `Simplify["x+-x--x"]`. |
| `Simplify(list)` | `CmdSimplify` takes a function, a `GeoFunctionable` or a text; a list is an illegal argument, like a point or a polygon. | Refused with an error. | The same refusal as the reference, recorded by the `reject` steps of `cas-rejected-004`. |
| Other symbolic argument types | The algebra input uses Giac for solving, `PlotSolve`, and rewrites of functions, curves, implicit curves and conic or line graphs. | With the `giac` feature and the pinned library, the kernel translates those arguments and reads back the resulting geometry. The default CAS covers bounded polynomial and rational functions. | The command templates, value text and read-back rules are documented in [docs/cas-giac.md](docs/cas-giac.md). The feature overlay records the remaining case-level differences. |
| CAS arithmetic and work limits | Precision and time depend on the CAS backend and application settings. | Coefficients are reduced signed 128-bit fractions. Expansion uses total degree 64 and 512 nonzero monomials. Univariate factorization stops above degree 32 and splits `x^n - 1` and `x^n + 1` through degree 32 by cyclotomic polynomials, peels rational roots before any modular work, proves irreducibility by a prime whose reduction is irreducible (Gauss's lemma) and prunes the subset recombination by the constant coefficients a factor may take. Multivariate factorization uses at most three variables, encoded degree 24 and 4,096 recombination candidates per split. Univariate root isolation stops above degree 32. Small systems are limited to three variables, eight equations, 24 basis elements, total degree 16, 256 terms per basis element and 256 real tuples. Prime neighbors are undefined only above the largest exact integer of a double, answer 2 below 2 like the CAS, and between 10^15 and that largest integer they answer the 15-digit mantissa times 10^11 that GeoGebra's display hands the CAS, where neighboring primes are indistinguishable. | Checked arithmetic, the kernel expression meter and a per-command allowance of 500,000 work units bound recomputation. An operation past its bound is undefined. The rational cancellation path can still remove shared monomials if the multivariate factor search runs out of its search range. |

The factorization uses [Yun's square-free decomposition](https://doi.org/10.1145/800205.806320),
modular Berlekamp factorization and [Zassenhaus's Hensel lifting](https://doi.org/10.1016/0022-314X(69)90047-X). Multivariate
factors are reconstructed using mixed-radix Kronecker substitution and checked
by exact sparse division. The small-system path uses [Buchberger's algorithm](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsc.2005.09.007)
for lexicographic Gröbner elimination and bounded real back-substitution. No arbitrary-precision crate
is needed within the stated bounds.

## CAS view

| Setting | GeoGebra | This project |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CAS assignments (`casview-closing-001`) | `a := 2` prints `a:=2`, binds `a` and makes the later `a + 1` evaluate to `3`. The equation `a = 2` leaves `a` unbound. | The stateless single-row evaluator prints `?` for `a := 2` and leaves `a + 1` symbolic. The equation agrees (`casview-closing-002`). |
| CAS whitespace (`casview-closing-007`, `-008`, `-009`) | The cell rewrites or trims runs of spaces in the recorded inputs. | The editor and importer keep the exact input. |
| CAS work limits (`casview-closing-008`, `-009`, `-011`) | The reference normalizes the padded row to one digit. Four references to that input print `4`, which it retains after the row changes. It opens the 16 KiB-plus row and the 1,001-row file without an evaluation warning. | The padded input is preserved, so the expanded row exceeds 16 KiB and prints `?`. An edit whose expanded copies would exceed 256 KiB is refused atomically. Files retain rows and saved outputs under the document-wide XML and item limits. Inputs beyond 16 KiB or rows past 1,000 open with a warning, and do not take part in evaluation or renumbering. Newly computed results above 4 KiB per representation display `?`. |
| CAS file presentation | A saved CAS cell can carry editing modes and comment styling. | The reader retains cell input and output and their expression attributes. Comment typography and tool-specific editing modes are outside the panel's current support. Native saved integer results have math formatting. Other native results are shown as saved text, with a bounded display preview for long results. |

## Numerical function analysis

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Root(function)`, `Roots(function)`, `Roots(function, number, number)`, `Extremum(function)`, `Extremum(function, number, number)`, `TurningPoint(function)`, `ComplexRoot(function)`, `RemovableDiscontinuity(function)` | An algorithm can create more labelled outputs after an edit. | Existing slots recompute in order and become undefined when the corresponding result disappears. Additional labels are allocated at creation. | Growing the output list requires journal support. The kernel keeps a stable set of objects for replay. |
| `ComplexRoot(function)` | Uses complex-number display and CAS-aware polynomial expansion. | Returns points whose coordinates are the real and imaginary parts of the numeric polynomial roots. | The geometry kernel represents the two coordinates without a separate complex number kind. |
| `Function(function, any, number, number)` | A standalone bound variable names the restricted function's local argument. | Accepts an identifier as the bound variable, with inclusive endpoints. | The argument is a binding and must be a valid name. |
| `Function(list)` as the input of other commands | Reads the freehand operation as an expression node. Bounded intersection samples it, and `First(function, number)` lists its points. | Stores the interpolation data outside the expression arena. Evaluation, integration, length, extrema, root and tangent samples agree where recorded, and so do `First(function, number)` (`AlgoFunctionFreehand.getPoints`) and the bounded intersection, which samples it as it samples a chart function (`funcrest-freehandreaders-010`). | The interpolation data has no expression node, so every reader samples it. Cases `funcrest-freehandpoints-001` to `-005` record the point reader. |
| `Iteration(function, any, ...)`, `IterationList(function, any, ...)` | Local variables can carry arbitrary objects and may use symbolic substitution. | Local recurrences support numeric, point and boolean values with homogeneous seeds. The standard one- and two-variable numeric forms are also supported. | Both commands share the expression work budget. `IterationList` also has the list element limit of 2²⁰, including the initial value. |
| `Asymptote(conic)` | Computes conic asymptotes without CAS. | Computes conic asymptotes. | The Java harness verifies the hyperbola case in `relationreview-asymptotes-001`. |
| `Asymptote(function)` | Uses CAS limits and solves for poles. | Finds poles and horizontal or oblique limits of rational functions and of exp, atan or log with affine arguments. | The rational case is checked in `cas-delegated-001`. General symbolic limits remain outside the numeric solver. |
| `Asymptote(implicit)` | Finds directions and offsets from the homogeneous terms. | Uses the polynomial in each direction at infinity and returns the lines in reference order. | The list stays undefined after the input becomes undefined, as recorded in the recovery case. |
| `RemovableDiscontinuity(function)` | Solves denominators and takes limits with CAS. | Finds rational holes by polynomial multiplicity. For other expressions with a polynomial denominator, it estimates a two-sided limit at each denominator root. | The finite rational hole is checked in `cas-delegated-002`. Other expressions keep the bounded numerical path. |
| `NSolveODE(list, number, list, number)` | Uses Dormand–Prince 5(4) and records the accepted steps. | Integrates once per input set and recomputation, sharing accepted states across output slots. Supports up to 16 coupled states and 99,999 trials after the initial state. The outputs are undefined if the solve runs out of trials or work before reaching the end. | The limit bounds memory and work. With `y'(t,y) = 1`, the Java harness records 200,002 points for `NSolveODE({y'},0,{1},2000)`, including the initial point and the endpoint at time 2000. The kernel returns an undefined locus for this input. |
| Numeric `SolveODE` | Uses classical RK4. A failure during integration leaves a defined locus containing the points already recorded. | Keeps the initial point and any completed steps when integration stops. The kernel caps the trace at 100,000 points and also stops on work-budget exhaustion. | Invalid inputs such as a zero step still give an undefined locus. |
| Provenance of `SVD(list)` | The reference delegates the decomposition to a bundled implementation. `AlgoSVD` marks its result undefined for good once the input is undefined or not a matrix (`GeoList.isMatrix`), and `AlgebraProcessor.processList` makes a constant list whose rows hold only undefined elements undefined. | The Golub-Kahan-Reinsch algorithm in the formulation of the public-domain JAMA package: a matrix with fewer rows than columns is decomposed transposed, norms accumulate with fdlibm's `hypot` (Java's `Math.hypot`), and a `NaN` superdiagonal entry counts as negligible, so `NaN` and infinite entries still give factors. The undefined state is kept per expression tree and forgotten by a new definition; a locus puts it back after sampling, as the reference samples a copy of the construction, while an optimizer's trials keep it, as the reference's update the live objects. The rule for constant lists of undefined rows is applied to the argument of `SVD`; an object defined by such a list, such as `l = {{0/0}}`, is still a defined list in this kernel. | The conventions were fixed from black-box recordings: the `funcrest-svd` topic records signs, rank-deficient null spaces, 72 random and decimal matrices of twelve shapes, nonfinite entries, the undefined state and a dense 200 by 200 input, and all agree. The bundled source was not used. |
| Provenance of the `Rate` root search | The reference brackets the cash-flow equation and delegates the numerical root solve. | Independent bisection and bounded Newton iteration based on [NIST DLMF §3.8](https://dlmf.nist.gov/3.8). The annuity equation uses a stable evaluation near zero. | Java harness cases verify regular and near-zero rates, both payment types, distant guesses and failures to converge. Two upstream t-pairs record different last printed digits for numerically agreeing rates. |
| Provenance of the minimum search (`Extremum`, `Maximize`, `Minimize`, `LowerSum` and `UpperSum`) | The reference delegates the numerical search to a bundled implementation. | Parabolic interpolation with a golden-section safeguard from [Brent (1973), chapter 5](https://maths-people.anu.edu.au/~brent/pub/pub011.html). The kernel uses divided differences, a score-ordered sample set and a step floor derived from machine epsilon. | The Java harness checks the resulting extrema and optimizer output within the standard tolerance. |
| Provenance of the optimizer search (`Maximize` and `Minimize`) | The reference delegates the search to a bundled implementation. | Brent's method from the published `fmin` procedure (Brent 1973, chapter 5; Forsythe, Malcolm and Moler 1977), with the details reproduced from the reference's recorded sample sequences: the step floor `sqrt(1.2e-16) |x| + tol / 3`, a zero step taken downwards, a parabola with an undefined coefficient refused, a sample equal to the best one moving there and narrowing the interval to both samples, no result after the first undefined sample or 101 iterations. | The sample sequences and results agreed bit for bit on 63 000 random objectives and intervals recorded from the reference, and on the circle cases. `Extremum`, `LowerSum` and `UpperSum` keep the search of the row above. |
| `Slider` with expression endpoints | `CmdSlider` installs the endpoint objects. `GeoNumeric.resolveMinMax` updates dependents only if resolving the interval changes the slider value. The endpoint objects count as optimizer bounds even while their values are undefined. | Accepts live numeric endpoints in the command. A finite interval clamps the slider, a reversed finite interval makes it undefined, and recovery starts from 1 clamped to the new range. Optimizers using an undefined endpoint recover on the next objective or slider update. Redefinition reuses the free number, installs the lower bound first, and accepts a self-referential endpoint. Such an endpoint is read on a set without adding a circular listener edge. The importer restores live endpoint expressions, including forward references installed after reading the rest of the file. The optional increment and speed accept numeric expressions and are written to the slider’s animation tag. A slider redefinition that omits a command-supplied increment saves its current value. | `sliderbounds-clamp-001` and `misc-optimizebounds-001` record the clamp and recovery rules. |
| Start point of a clipped contour (`misc-alias-003`) | The sweep of the clip starts the intersection contour at the crossing of the boundaries. | The contour is emitted from its topmost point, which also moves the first output point. | The contours agree geometrically; the recorded case is listed in `known-gaps.toml`. |
| `SlowPlot(function)`, `SlowPlot(function, number)`, `SlowPlot(line)`, `SlowPlot(line, number)` | Hides the source graph and creates a labelled slider from 0 to 1 that plays at once. A new number has an automatic increment, 0.001 for this interval while it plays. The repeat value, cut to an integer, repeats at 1 and plays once otherwise. The animation manager steps at most 30 frames a second and lowers the rate when frames take long. The slider is placed on the screen below the previous one. | Hides the source graph when the line runs and plays the slider with the same step, increment and modes. The window steps a frame every thirtieth of a second at the rate the elapsed time gives, from 6 to 30 frames a second. The export writes the visibility and the animation tag as the reference does, and `startAnimation` while the animation runs; the importer plays a slider saved as playing again and starts it when the file says so. A play and pause button shows while a slider animates, and a rebuild of the journal (a deletion, undo or redo) keeps the slider values and playing flags, as a reference rebuild from its file does. | The kernel steps a frame at the rate it is given; adapting the rate to the drawing time and placing the slider are the window's. `funcrest-slowplot-007` to `funcrest-slowplot-012` record frames, both modes, the stop, values set by hand and recovery; `saved-slowplot-001` the visibility and animation tags. A script that reloads a document runs `SlowPlot` again, which hides the source again. The headless reference never runs its timer, so `startAnimation` follows `Kernel.getKernelXML` and `MyXMLHandler`; an imported animation whose step or speed is an expression does not play. |
| `nCr` and `nPr` past the exact range | The reference uses arbitrary-precision integer products. When the decimal of `n - r` equals that of `n`, the permutation product is empty and returns 1. The binomial command rejects inputs that differ from their saturated Java rounding, including `nCr(10^40, 2)`. | Products use `u128` while the intermediate values fit and use an `f64` product after overflow. A product longer than 100,000 factors uses the rounded log-gamma estimate. The empty permutation and rejected binomial cases still give 1 and 0 respectively. | The bound caps the work on each update. Products above the exact range can differ in their final rounding. |
| Empty result of a polygon clip (`IntersectPath` and the deprecated aliases) | A disjoint clip keeps one output point and one output segment. The point has a label. | A disjoint clip now creates one labelled undefined point alongside the polygon result. The point can become defined if the polygons overlap later. | The kernel allocates labelled outputs at creation and leaves out the unlabelled segment. See `misc-alias-003`. |
| `SetConstructionStep(number)` | Moves the protocol step and hides objects beyond it through view notifications. | Moves the protocol step, recomputes `ConstructionStep` readers and inserts subsequently created elements at the selected position. The workbench journals the change for replay and undo. | Protocol navigation works headlessly. The `misc-protocol` cases cover rounding, clamping, insertion and later changes to the argument. Step-based visibility belongs to the view. |
| `AxisStepX`, `AxisStepY` | Read the graphics view's grid distances, which can be fixed by the user or expressed in pi units. | Derive the automatic 1–2–5 numbering interval independently from each axis's screen scale. Panning, zooming and workbench rebuilds refresh the algebra values of view readers and their dependents. | The kernel supports automatic intervals. Custom grid distances and pi-unit axes are outside the current view model. |
| `Maximize`/`Minimize` cost | Each trial sets the mover and cascades its update. An optimizer guards its own recursion. After copying the result and restoring the mover, it updates the construction with the guard still set. | Each search makes at most 102 objective evaluations. Affected optimizers rerun during a trial, each cascading from its own mover. The final update of the mover can make a point on an undefined circle undefined again, even while the circle keeps its geometry for the trials. The optimizer whose trial is interrupted this way can return the point's previous coordinates, as in `misc-maximizepoint-019`. | Shared searches stop with an undefined result after 16,384 dependency edges, eight active searches, 1,000,000 descendant visits at any one depth, or 80,000,000 expression work units. The final update refreshes optimizers on other movers in dependency order, considering the first 256 optimizer definitions. It shares an 80,000,000-unit expression budget across searches and descendants and charges each descendant visit. The cap limits the cost of one final update, and pending descendants keep their previous values if the sweep stops. Trial and rollback work share the search budget, including scalar expression visits. When a cascade detects that the shared deadline is exhausted, rollback reinstates saved results without rerunning their expressions. A reader that refuses a bulk charge under the shared deadline stops the cascade and triggers the same rollback. If the refusal occurs while updating readers after the search has found an optimum, the optimizer keeps that result. In the 20-reader stress construction formerly recorded as `optimizerreview-budget-002`, GeoGebra returns 2 for the second optimizer. The kernel reaches the shared deadline, returns an undefined second optimizer, and restores the mover, the first optimum of 3, and the previous reader values. A Rust regression covers this deliberate work-limit difference. Isolated searches recompute their objective chain, and locus sampling holds the current optimum. The two locus samples with extra wrap points remain in `known-gaps.toml`. |
| Locus sampling work | Stops when a single update of the sampled construction takes more than 500 ms. | The sampling sweep uses the shared expression budget, capped at 80,000,000 work units. The sweep reserves work to save and restore live expression results before sampling. Each cache miss charges one unit for each object visited in the dependency chain, in addition to metered expression work and the work of any sampled minimum spanning tree. Exhaustion makes the locus undefined and restores the live construction from the saved state. The repeated 200,000-element Sequence in `locus-samplingbudget-002` exhausts the meter, while the reference returns 149 samples. | The deterministic budget avoids machine-dependent geometry and caps aggregate work across trial positions. |
| Slider with an endpoint expression that depends on the slider (`optimizerreview-redefine-008`) | Keeps the listener and resolves the endpoint when an independent input of its expression changes, including after loading a saved file. | During redefinition the self-dependent endpoint is read without an edge back to the slider. An edit to another input of the expression leaves the slider and its descendants at their previous values. | Restoring this listener requires a cycle-aware update order and a stopping rule for feedback. |
| Two slider intervals changed within one edit to a locus input (`optimizerreview-locus-004`) | The clamp of the first slider cascades before the second slider's endpoint updates. The locus samples with the previous interval and samples the new interval when its mover changes. | Endpoint values are applied in order. The point and locus update after the endpoints and see the new interval in the first sweep. | An intermediate locus sample would require running its descendant cascade before the next endpoint algorithm and preserving the update order when that cascade returns. |
| Nested optimizer final updates involving a third mover (`optimizerreview-mixedmover-002`) | The nested search restores its mover and updates the construction while both active searches keep their guards. Another mover's optimizer can change before the outer objective is read. | A nested search refreshes its mover's descendants. The outer search refreshes optimizers on other movers after its trials. | A construction-wide sweep during trials would need to share the active guards, rollback state and work budget across all movers it can reach. |

The historical numerical analysis group still uses the dummy CAS. The
native CAS group verifies rational asymptotes and finite holes separately.
The dummy backend leaves the sine curvature vector undefined in the
historical group. The kernel evaluates it numerically.

`Roots` samples both explicit intervals and the active sampling window,
even for polynomials. It uses the construction's horizontal view scale and
falls back to the reference headless view when no view is supplied.
Unrestricted `Extremum(function)` samples the view when the expanded
degree exceeds 25. Three-argument `If` uses the numerical path in
`Root`, `Roots` and `Extremum`. The reference `TurningPoint` still
requires a polynomial or a supported restricted polynomial.

`Root(function)` keeps products and integer powers factored when possible.
Dense polynomial expansion and Laguerre deflation are work-budgeted.
Expansion bounds each power at 300, the reference parser's bound for literal
powers. The work budget governs the overall expanded degree.
The real polynomial command limits Laguerre to 100 evaluations per factor
and keeps exact zero roots if deflation fails, as the reference does.

The bracketed solver introduced in `b95f406` had tolerance defaults and
control flow consistent with the reference's third-party solver. This
revision implements the bracket updates and inverse interpolation from
[Brent, *Algorithms for Minimization without Derivatives* (1973), chapter 4](https://maths-people.anu.edu.au/brent/pub/pub011.html).
The EUPL command classes supply the seed and endpoint ordering,
defined-domain retry, and accuracy settings. The Java harness checks the
returned roots and Newton polishing, including when Brent returns an
endpoint.

The Runge–Kutta formulas come from [Dormand and Prince, *A family of
embedded Runge-Kutta formulae* (1980), table 2](https://doi.org/10.1016/0771-050X(80)90013-3)
and [Hairer, Nørsett and Wanner, *Solving Ordinary Differential Equations I*,
chapter II](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-78862-1), which describes
classical RK4 and adaptive step size selection. The Java harness compares
step times, accepted states and endpoints.

Curvature of general function and parametric inputs uses a five-point
finite difference of the kernel's first derivative where a second derivative
is needed.

## Remaining transformations, equation sides and conditionals

| Area | GeoGebra | Kernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Dilate(list, number)` with polygon or Cartesian curve elements (`matrixdilate-dilateleaves-001` and `-002`) | Transforms each polygon or curve and stores it as a list element. | A list beginning with either leaf kind produces an undefined dilation. | Expression list slots lack records for transformed polygon vertices and transformed Cartesian curve definitions and parameter intervals. |
| Work budget for list dilation of conditional conics (`matrixdilate-dilatecarrier-001`) | Follows the active conditional branches, then transforms their selected circle or conic. | Charges each active branch to the enclosing list evaluation. The expression is undefined if the remaining allowance runs out. | The work of traversing conditional carriers counts toward the existing expression work limit. |
| `Dilate(image, number)` and `Dilate(image, number, point)` | Maps three effective image corners. A screen-fixed image keeps its placement. | Maps the same corners and shares the source raster. Transformed image chains deeper than 32 are undefined. | The depth limit bounds the work to resolve placement, including corners derived from the view scale. |
| `ApplyMatrix(list, image)` | Maps three effective image anchors, with homogeneous division for a 3 by 3 matrix. The fourth corner follows from the three anchors. | Maps those anchors and keeps each finite corner available when another reaches infinity. The image draws only when its corners are finite and enclose nonzero area. | The raster frame needs a finite, nondegenerate parallelogram to draw. Placement follows the same depth bound as image dilation. |
| `ApplyMatrix(list, function)` | `GeoFunction.toGeoCurveCartesian` uses an explicit interval if present. Other graphs use the active view, with a range of `[-10, 10]` when its horizontal bounds coincide. | Preserves explicit intervals from legacy saved functions and uses the same rule with the configured sampling view for other functions. | The desktop currently supplies its view to the locus sampling path. A construction without a locus can retain the headless window. |
| `LeftSide(implicit)` and `RightSide(implicit)` | Evaluate the selected side of the displayed equation as a function of two variables. | Use the typed sides in input form and the polynomial terms in implicit form. | The sides become undefined with the equation and recover when it does. |
| `CommonDenominator(function, function)` | Computes a common denominator through the symbolic backend. The recorded test with an empty backend returns an undefined function. | Computes exact common denominators for supported polynomial quotients. | The test backend leaves the function undefined even when the polynomial quotient can be normalized. |
| `If(boolean, any, ...)` | Chooses an output class in argument order (`GeoList.getGenericElement`) and copies the selected value into it. `AlgoIf.compute` applies the else alternative, or makes the output undefined when there is none, at every false condition before it tests the next. A segment output copies a line's coefficients and keeps its end points, length and defined flag; a line output copies a segment's coefficients. | Follows argument order for recorded scalar, list, text and point alternatives. Same-kind geometry alternatives carry their geometry records, including inline single-output commands. Segment and line alternatives mix as in the reference: a segment output starts from a copy of the generic element, keeps the copied line coefficients beside its value, and replays the loop's fallbacks, so a segment made undefined stays undefined until a segment is selected. The command accepts up to 64 alternatives. | Readers of the segment's coefficients (perpendicular and parallel lines, mirrors, angles, line distances and `Distance(point, segment)`, which measures to the `AlgoClosestPoint` result after its two `pointChanged` passes) see the copied line, and a segment output that selects such a segment copies its coefficients too; readers that use the segment as a path or by its end points use the kept end points. Recorded in `funcrest-conditionalmixed-001` to `funcrest-conditionalmixed-005`. Arithmetic on a list result can read the kinds of the elements retained by an undefined branch. |

## Construction export

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Object kinds | Formula, implicit curve, pen stroke and turtle objects can be saved. | Files contain objects represented by the kernel. Axes are saved as view settings. A LaTeX text is a text element. Implicit polynomials use an implicit element with a coefficient array. | The kind matrix records the classes available in the kernel. |
| Fixed-length segment | The endpoint is a point on an anonymous circle. The segment command takes the start point and that endpoint. | The writer reconstructs the two commands from the fixed-path state and uses the accepted inline start expression when there is no public start label. On import, the endpoint's saved coordinates select its position on the anonymous circle. | The command sequence preserves the original labels and the endpoint's dependence on the starting point and length. |
| Transformed curves and intersections | Saves transformed curves and curve intersections as commands with their input references and output slots. | The writer emits native `Dilate`, `ApplyMatrix` and `Intersect` commands for supported curve forms. It retains contiguous, labelled line–curve intersection slots created by edits, including slots that have since become undefined. A point on a curve carries its raw parameter to identify its branch. | The reload tests check transformed curves, curve intersections and saved curve parameters. Export refuses gaps in curve intersection slots to keep labels attached to their original roots. |
| Command forms and history | A file can carry growing output groups, intersection branch history, and generated helpers for native commands. | The writer uses live definitions for supported commands with labelled inputs. The original construction source and accepted file definitions share a 16 MiB payload budget. The journal supplies output grouping and serves as a fallback when optional source retention is exhausted. A spreadsheet fill reserves the destination formulas before changing any cells. History-dependent forms, such as a regular polygon whose vertex count changed, may reopen with different output slots. Other kernel-only definitions, including replay slots for polygonal boundary intersections, are refused. | The two kernels use different representations of command state. The export gate covers representative cases by kind. |
| Appearance of an object from a file | Every element saves its presentation as tags; a tag left out keeps the default of the reader. | The importer keeps these tags as the saved style of the object and the writer puts back exactly the tags the file had: visibility, the view bits and the condition, colour, opacity, fill pattern with angle, distance, symbol and inversion, dynamic colour, background, layer and drawing order, label visibility, mode and offset, caption and dynamic caption, tooltip mode, line thickness, dash pattern, opacity and hidden pattern, point size and style, the fixed, fixed-size, auxiliary, selection, trace, trimmed-lines, breakpoint and algebra-label flags, decoration, the end styles of a segment or broken line, the head of a vector, text font, LaTeX, rounding and placement, angle style, arc size and right-angle emphasis, slider bounds, placement, width, knob size, orientation, lock and algebra visibility, animation step, speed, mode and state, the lock of a check box, and the position and length of an input box. | The export oracle loads the source file and the export of its import in the reference application and compares each property of every object. `crates/geo-golden/tests/fixtures/export-proof.json` names, for every kind and property, the object whose saved value differs from what the reader gives it without the tag. |
| Appearance of an object made in the application | A new object takes the construction defaults of its class. | The writer stores what the canvas shows: the palette colour, an opacity of 0.1 for polygons and angle sectors and 0 otherwise, visibility, layer, label visibility and offset, line thickness, a full line, and each slider’s screen position and name-and-value label mode. Other tags are left out. | The editor has no control for the other properties, so the reader's defaults apply. |
| Saved style and editing | A style belongs to its object for the whole session. | The saved style is kept beside the document, by label. The canvas applies layers, label offsets, label visibility and content, saved colour, stroke thickness and opacity, straight-line dash patterns, point size and shape, and text size, bold and italic. Its palette provides the default colour. Other saved style fields remain available for export. Deleting an object drops its imported style record and undo restores that record. A snapshot script does not carry the record. A fixed object can still be dragged. | The kernel has no fixed flag and the canvas applies only the presentation fields it draws. A reused label must not inherit the style of a deleted object. |
| Dynamic colour, animation step and speed | The reader passes these attributes to its command processor. It keeps an expression whose inputs are undefined: an undefined colour channel gives 0, and an undefined speed counts as 0. | They are kept when the kernel evaluates them as numeric arithmetic over the construction, whatever their current value, and written in the kernel's syntax: a channel `x(Q) / 10` or a step `sqrt(a)` survives import and export while `Q` is undefined or `a` is negative (`saved-styles-012`). A tree with anything the kernel parses without evaluating it, such as a list command, a text or a script or assignment command, is dropped on import with a note that names the object, and dropped on export once an object it names is gone: a speed of `Limit[x^2, 2]` reloads as 1. The static colour stays. All such expressions of a document share one evaluation budget; past it the rest is dropped with the same note. | A file must not carry text for the reader's command processor that the kernel does not evaluate as arithmetic. The kernel has no limit command. |
