| Rounding of one text | A dependent text prints its numbers with its own `<decimals>` or `<significantfigures>`. | The tag is kept and written back. The kernel prints the text with the rounding of the document. | Text templates take the document rounding; a per-object format is not implemented. The style fixtures use whole numbers, which both print alike. |
| Additional element presentation | The object classes write their display properties with per-class defaults, units and legacy attribute forms. | The imported document retains the original attribute values and relative order of the additional properties it writes, including InputBox font, symbolic mode, alignment and temporary input, list font, angle and drop-down settings, vector start point, number and text symbolic mode, function display and table settings, legacy positioning, and individual bar and pie slice styles. Legacy check box and slider positions use the live control fields and are saved as a label offset or slider location. The source GUI font size accompanies legacy widget fonts. Button dimensions and fixed-size flags keep their relative order. The original class gates reuse. Conic eigenvectors precede the matrix. | These records are file presentation. The app still uses its existing editor and drawing model. The tag witnesses identify the actual source, export and GeoGebra reload values. |
| Inline text, mind maps and media | Inline text and embeds can have `verticalAlign`, `content`, `contentSize`, `borderColor`, `boundingBox`, `dimensions angle`, `parent`, `audio`, `video`, `embed` and `embedSettings`. | The exporter handles ordinary Text and raster images. It does not convert a legacy Text with `boundingBox` into an inline object and cannot write inline rotation, mind maps, audio, video or embeds. Raster rotation is encoded by the corners of an image. | The construction has no inline text, mind-map or media kind. |
| Legacy and ancillary properties | Older files may use `linkedGeo exp` for an InputBox, `allowReflexAngle` and `forceReflexAngle` for angles, and `strokeCoords` for vertices of a stroke. GeoGebra also writes `variables` for symbolic objects, `casMap` for cached results and `listener` for JavaScript callback names. | A link is written as a native Textfield command input. A legacy link declared solely by `linkedGeo` is not restored. The angle flags pass through in order with angleStyle and update the live angle. The importer applies bounded styles to the raw value where the free angle was read, so the current style can precede the normalized value on save. Accepted legacy stroke vertices are written as `strokeBezierCoords`. CAS caches, symbolic-object variables and listener registrations are omitted. | Command inputs name objects. The export file disables scripting, and the geometry model has no symbolic-object CAS state. |
| Saved start points and numeric presentation expressions | The reference evaluates expressions in start points and vertical increments. | The export writes a vector's saved coordinates, named point or supported dependent point expression. Deleting a point that an anchor depends on detaches the anchor. Undo restores it. The vector still draws at its existing kernel origin. A vertical increment retains supported numeric arithmetic, lengths of named lists used as scalar operands, and a standalone sum of one named list that the kernel types as numeric or empty. List expressions retain the reference's brackets. An explicit bracketed length of an exact list label survives an object named `Length`. An explicit bracketed standalone sum of an exact list label survives an object named `Sum`. Standalone lengths use the canonical label when it fits the XML attribute limit. Export omits an increment while a required list label is absent or names another kind. | Both expressions use the shared style budget. List lengths combine with scalar unary and binary operations. A general-typed list can hold numeric values and still be ineligible for a sum. List literals and inline list-producing expressions as operands, conditionals and user-defined function calls involving list lengths, sums embedded in arithmetic, multi-argument sums, and other list commands are omitted with an import note. |
| Image-backed presentation | The archive can contain image fills, widget icons and images for chart bars or slices. | Bar and slice images and widget icons share the PNG, JPEG and GIF asset limits of Image objects. The export preserves their bytes under generated archive names and refuses a save that would exceed the combined raster memory or decode limit on reopen. Only the last image name for each bar or slice is resolved. GeoGebra retains a missing image name in its XML. The importer reports it and omits the reference without reviving an earlier texture. Oversized or unsupported assets are also omitted with a note. Valid per-bar colors, alpha, hatch and symbol attributes pass through. | SVG and built-in widget icons have no supported archive asset. Object-wide image fills still have no export path. |
| Explicit plain text on a LaTeX command result | Reloading an element with `isLaTeX=false` on a `LaTeX` command result enables LaTeX again. | Rust keeps the explicit plain-text override when saving and reopening its own files. | The defining expression supplies the default mode in Rust. An explicit saved mode takes precedence. |
| Spreadsheet one-variable analysis | Classic opens a source confirmation dialog before displaying the analysis view. | Select a numeric row or column of up to 256 cells and open the read-only summary directly from the sheet. Use Shift-click or the range toggle followed by a cell tap to extend the selection. The summary keeps the launched range and follows its values. Short windows give the grid the available space and keep the summary close action in the header. | The first slice covers the raw statistics table. The reference witnesses capture the shared data source and statistics models. |
| Spreadsheet tracing and compatibility tags | The desktop writer can save spreadsheet tracing settings. Older tags also encode reflex angles, fading and automatic colour. | Standard trace attributes within the sheet coordinate limits pass through without enabling tracing in the app. The legacy angle flags, `fading` and `autocolor` also pass through. | The headless writer supplies no spreadsheet trace XML, modern angle files use `angleStyle`, and no fixture witnesses `fading` or `autocolor`. |
| InputBox temporary edits | A linked field can save separate `display` and `eval` inputs. When the reference reloads and resaves `saved-element-properties-001` headlessly, both attributes are absent. | The original and the exported XML carry both attributes exactly. The reference resave omits them after reloading either file. An empty unlinked field provides the unchanged reload witness for `tempUserInput`. | The linked-field fixture demonstrates preservation in the export XML. |
| Other definition attributes | `value random`, implicit `userinput value`, and quadric matrix slots can change the definition or initial value. | The writer uses the supported kernel value, implicit equation form and conic matrix. It omits the random flag and implicit input expression, and does not export 3D quadric elements. | The export regenerates modeled definitions. It does not copy executable input into implicit-object attributes. |
| Object without a `<show>` tag | The reader hides an object that its element tag creates until a `<show object="true">` tag. | The importer shows every object without the tag. While the object stays visible the writer leaves the tag out again, so the reference application reads the export as it reads the source. | Files written by the reference application carry the tag for every drawable object. The difference concerns hand-written files. |
| `<show>` tag without `object="true"` | Only the text `true` shows the object, and the label is hidden unless `label="true"`. | The importer follows this. Before, a tag without the attribute, or with any text but `false`, showed the object: a point saved as `<show label="true"/>` was visible and is hidden now. | A compatibility change towards the reference reader, covered by `hand-show-without-object` and an importer test. |
| Free number made in the application | A number shown in the graphics view is a slider. The reader gives it the interval from -5 to 5 and moves its value into it. | The writer leaves `<show>` out for a visible free number without bounds: the canvas draws nothing for it, and the reference application keeps its value (`export-numbers-001`). A slider with bounds is written as shown. | A shown number of 400 would reload as 5. |
| Half-open slider interval | A visible slider whose file gives one endpoint gets a default for the other when the file is read. | The writer marks the missing endpoint as undefined, so the reloaded slider has the interval it had when it was saved. | The kernel keeps the saved state, which the reference application itself changes on reload. |
| Order of the tags of a number | The reader gives a number that becomes visible an interval around its current value, so its files hold the value and the slider before `<show>`. | The writer uses that order. With `<show>` first the value was still the default, the interval became -5 to 5, and a slider saved at 30 on 0 to 100 reloaded at 5. | `export-sliders-002`, `saved-styles-011` and `hand-slider-value-outside` hold such sliders. |
| Malformed style tags | The reader applies the attributes of a tag in a fixed order and stops at the first number it cannot read. A `<lineStyle>` needs its dash pattern before the thickness; a `<slider>` without a width keeps its bounds and position and loses the flags after it. In `<objColor>` the hatch angle, the hatch distance, the fill pattern and the opacity are read outside any guard: a missing or malformed one makes the reader give up the whole file. Colour channels are masked to their low byte and a line may have any thickness. | The importer reads in the same order and keeps what the tag had set by then. Where the reference gives up the file, the importer reads on, keeps the part of the tag that was applied and says so in a note; the export then holds a well-formed tag. A tag the reference read halfway is written as far as it was read. | The hand-written files of `crates/geo-golden/tests/fixtures/export` are loaded by the reference application with its reports recorded. The export of a file never makes the reader report more than the source did. |
| Views | `<show ev=>` puts an object into the first or second graphics view, the 3D view and the view of a plane. | The bits are kept and written back. The editor has one view and draws every visible object in it. | The application has one graphics view. |
| Layer of an object without `<layer>` | A new object takes the highest layer used so far in the view, so the layer of an object without the tag depends on the objects before it. | The absence of the tag is kept and elements are written in the order of the journal, which is the order of the file. In the editor such an object is on layer 0. | Files written by the reference application carry the tag for every drawable object. |
| Algebra label of a free object | The reader shows the algebra label again when it names an object that only its element tag creates. | The writer saves a free point, a free number, a button and an image as a bare element, whatever the file did, so a hidden algebra label of such an object is shown after the export. For an object of a command or an expression the flag is kept. | The reference application itself loses the flag for an image or a button. A free point typed as an expression keeps it there and loses it here. |
| Arrows of a slope field | `<lineStyle drawArrow=>` draws the locus of a slope field as arrows. The same setting has no effect on a pen stroke. | The exporter retains the attribute on a slope field. The oracle compares the full arrow geometry between both reference reloads and all 1,600 pairs of shaft endpoints against the live and reopened kernel. | The reference adds three arrowhead vertices to each two-point shaft. The kernel locus stores shafts. |
| Reader rules for old formats | For a file of format 3.19 or older the opacity of a list is not read. Below format 3.3 a point takes the point style of the document, also for `<pointStyle val="-1">`. | The list rule is followed. The point style of an old document is not: `-1` is kept and written into a file of the current format, where it means the default of the view. | Files of 2009 and earlier. |
| Graphics and worksheet settings | Multiple views, view-dependent bounds, per-axis options, grid spacing, dock layout and worksheet metadata can be saved. | The primary view stores origin, size, two scales, axis visibility and grid visibility. The app has one axes toggle. Kernel rounding and angle unit pass through the file. | The application uses one graphics view and automatic ticks. |
| Archive extras | Optional thumbnails, construction defaults, macros and non-raster assets can be included. | The archive includes construction XML and referenced PNG, JPEG or GIF bytes. It omits the JavaScript library because GeoGebra can run its initialization function even when object scripting is disabled. Per-object scripts are retained with scripting disabled. | The application stores raster assets and treats imported scripts as data. |
| Deleting the first output of `Point(list)` after more coordinate rows appear | Keeps the later row labels and their values. | Removes the group with its first output (`keptvalues-pointgrowth-003`). | The journal keys the growth group and its appended slot definitions to the first label. Keeping the later points requires a group owner that survives deleting that label. |
| `Point(list)` with a list of paths | Uses the list as a path. A point on a one-element list follows the element and recovers when the element becomes defined again. | Supports coordinate rows. A list literal containing an inline path command is refused, and a named list of paths cannot be used as a path (`keptvalues-pointpath-001`). | The path machinery takes one object ID. A list path needs persistent element identities and a parameter that selects both an element and its position on that element. |
| Circles with a Cartesian-infinite centre | The circle is defined and its half-axis stores the radius. For a circle through a finite point this radius is infinite. | Defined, Radius and RemoveUndefined see the retained value. The finite geometry interface continues to omit these circles. | Drawing and geometric algorithms use finite centres. |

## Spreadsheet

| Area | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Spreadsheet work per edit or command | The sheet can address 9,999 rows and columns. The fill and range commands can work across the full sheet. | The address bounds are the same. A command can visit at most 1,024 distinct cells. A fill shares this allowance across its destinations and their shifted inputs, and has additional bounds on speculative parsing, staged definitions, dependencies and copied text. A cell edit has a 16 KiB input limit. | An edit or command is evaluated synchronously and must keep its work bounded. |
| Replacing a spreadsheet cell with a geometric construction | A range fill can replace existing cells with copies of an object such as a regular polygon. | Cell replacement uses the construction's in-place redefinition rules. A fill that would replace a cell with a multi-output geometric construction is refused before writing. | Retaining the cell label must also preserve the dependencies and output ownership of the construction. |
| Spreadsheet drag fills | A selection of several rows or columns can supply a repeating or linear pattern. | Dragging the handle fills from the active cell along one axis. Relative references shift and absolute references keep their row or column. | The grid has one active cell. |
| Spreadsheet lookup dependencies | Cell follows addresses as cells are created. | Cell observes the cells present at definition and reserves its initial target if empty. If an index later points at an address that was absent at definition, a new cell there can require another index update to refresh the lookup. A dynamic lookup watches the known slots once per expression and shares the 1,024-cell budget with ranges. The source address of a complex coordinate is reserved when it can be evaluated within the spreadsheet work budget. | The dependency graph binds known slots at construction time. |
| Clearing spreadsheet cells | Clearing an unprotected source removes its dependent objects. | The editor deletes a single-output cell and its dependents through the journal. A Cell lookup that only watches the removed cell survives and becomes undefined if that cell was selected. Ranges leave the removed member out and refresh when it is entered again. A kernel-only clear or a clear in a multi-cell fill entry leaves dependents in place. The slot becomes blank when the kernel can redefine it in place. | The kernel graph keeps stable object IDs. A multi-cell journal entry cannot yet drop one of its outputs. |
| Saving a spreadsheet fill | The file contains individual cell definitions. `FillRow(1,{7})` saves numeric `A1 = 7`. | Export writes the current definition of each resulting cell in dependency order. | The fill command changes the sheet once. The saved cells define the resulting state. |
| FillRow over an existing slider | Fills A1 with 7 and replaces B1 with 8 in `spreadsheet-fillslider-001`. | The fill is refused with A1 absent and the B1 slider intact. | In-place replacement of an existing slider requires slider bounds on the new definition. |
| Labelled FillRow changing an input's kind | `r = FillRow(1,{"text",A1^2})` with A1 initially 3 writes text to A1 and 9 to B1. | The fill is refused before writing. A1 stays 3, B1 stays absent, and r is absent. | Changing A1 to text would invalidate the numeric dependency of the labelled result. |
| Spreadsheet selection | Row and column headers select their whole axis, and dragging the fill handle selects the filled range. | Header labels do not select an axis. A drag leaves the active source cell selected. Preferred sizes, row and column sizes, and grid-line visibility are applied. Widths below 32 and heights below 16 points are clamped. | The editor stores one active cell. A minimum cell size keeps the number of visible widgets bounded. |
| Spreadsheet value strings | Defined geometric and angle objects use their own value strings. | The grid displays segment, polyline and arc lengths and polygon and sector areas. Degree angles use a degree suffix. Lists in radian mode append `rad` to angle elements. Equations of lines, circles and conics and degrees-minutes-seconds formatting remain unsupported in the grid. | The grid has scalar formatters and does not reuse the algebra equation renderer. |

## Graphics editor tools

| Tool | Reference | geokernel |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Show/Hide Label followed by a CAS edit | In `tool_label_then_cas_undo` the headless reference stores no checkpoint for the click while the label mode is active. Undo after the CAS submission and a switch to Move restores the original label visibility and removes the CAS row together. | A CAS edit closes the pending label step before recording its own step. In the same sequence the first undo removes the CAS row and leaves the label hidden, and the second undo shows the label. |
| Show/Hide Label on raster images | The headless image probe observes placement and cancellation. Image-label clicks were not recorded. | The label tool ignores raster images. |
| Copy Visual Style | Copies basic and advanced style, including the visibility condition and object-specific properties. | Copies static colour, layer, label visibility and stroke thickness. The source stays active until clicked again or the mode is left. Other presentation and conditional properties remain outside this editor. |
| Move Rotate | Rotates a pointer-changeable object or its free input points about the selected centre. The recorded headless gesture leaves no undo checkpoint. | Rotates a free point or the free input points of a movable object. A drag is one undo step. A candidate with more than 256 inputs or a centre-independence check requiring more than 2,048 dependency edges is refused. |
| Image | Classic opens a native chooser on toolbar activation and uses the selection to place the image. | Click the canvas or an existing point, then open the PNG, JPEG or GIF from the application's path dialog. The image and any new corners are one undo step. The pointer replay substitutes the fixture raster at the Java chooser boundary. Insertion reserves two ZIP entries for construction XML and optional spreadsheet metadata, so a construction can hold up to 4,094 assets through this tool. |
| Function Inspector | Opens a desktop inspector with a point table, derivative options and interval statistics. | Opens a table of x and f(x) with editable endpoints and increment. The initial nine samples are centred on the click. The table allows up to 256 rows and stops sampling at a shared limit of 65,536 expression work units. It reuses the samples until the interval or construction changes. Derivatives and interval statistics stay in the reference inspector. |
| Styled curves during anisotropic zoom | The reference redraws curves at their saved stroke thickness. | Kept mesh runs preserve their width when they have four vertices per sample. If a thin or sharp stroke requires tessellation after the frame has used its allowance, the kept mesh is scaled including its stroke width until a new plot fits the allowance. |
| Freehand Shape and Function | Tests segment and polygon candidates, then a circle, a conic and a function. Fits conics by singular value decomposition and can remember prior polygon edges. | Uses the reference's stroke moments, line and circle thresholds and interpolation. A single closed stroke can produce a triangle or quadrilateral. Ellipse and hyperbola fitting uses normalized least squares with a fixed constant. The numerical fits and borderline classification can differ, and a stroke that requires the reference's singular or parabolic conic fit still falls back to a function or creates nothing. Each stroke takes at most 512 samples and a function spans at most 2,048 pixel columns. |

## Object properties

| Area | Desktop behavior |
| --- | --- |
| Tabs | The five-tab inspector places slider controls in Style, Position and Advanced, equation controls in Advanced, and text controls in Color, Style and Position. Classic has separate Text, Slider and Algebra tabs. Scripting is a separate package. |
| Control coverage | Classic offers name and definition editors, point coordinate format, dynamic caption, trace, vertical increment, random sliders, angle decorations, and image corner editors. These controls are absent from this inspector. The slider fields require an installed numeric slider, so free numbers without a slider and free angles omit them. Circle and conic fill opacity and dash controls are absent. The conic equation menu offers general, specific and parametric forms. Classic conditionally offers explicit, input, vertex and conic forms as well. |
| Numeric fields | Linked numeric input boxes accept decimal counts from 0 to 15 and significant-figure counts from 1 to 15. Classic offers 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 10, 13 and 15 decimal places, 3, 5, 10 and 15 figures, and a reset to the document default. Native files and the existing numeric editor accept intermediate counts. Slider thickness is entered in pixels and stored in whole half-pixels, with a 127.5 px ceiling from the stored byte. Text accepts the reference's 1–10,000% range. The renderer still bounds font sizes to 4–256 px. |
| Position and animation fields | The inspector bounds position to ±10⁹, label offset to signed 16-bit values, slider length to 0.1–10,000, slider knob radius to 1–63.5, increment to 0–10⁹ and animation speed to ±100. Classic accepts evaluated numeric expressions for slider length, knob size, thickness, increment and speed. The inspector accepts literal numbers in those fields. |
| Vector head | Clearing the vector head checkbox removes the drawn triangle and writes head style 0. Classic draws the closed triangular head for style 0, including when the archive omits its default head tag. |
| Selection | Modifier-click in the algebra view extends the selection. Fields shared by the selection apply as a single history entry. Selections over 512 objects are refused before applying any change. Graph, formula and animation edits also have an 8,192-visit construction allowance. |
| Algebra and text | The marker distinguishes auxiliary objects in the flat algebra list. Per-object numeric rounding applies to linked numeric input boxes. Text renders saved size, bold and italic using the bundled font family. The serif setting is retained without selecting a different desktop font family. Canvas captions use up to 16 KiB of text at a UTF-8 boundary. Archives retain the full caption. |
| Save | Native snapshots keep edited properties. The construction archive uses the writer's presentation tags, including code 9 for caption and value. The dedicated slider-track color has no archive tag. |

## Construction tools

| Tool | GeoGebra | geokernel | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Relation | Numerical report with symbolic follow-up where available. | Numerical report with no construction change. | The toolbar uses the existing bounded numerical predicates. |
| Fixed Angle | Accepts a segment, including a side produced by a polygon algorithm. | A segment pick needs two named point endpoints. Two point picks work independently. | Computed segments without two endpoint IDs have no point objects for the angle's dependencies. |
| Rigid Polygon | A copy rotates when its second point moves. | The copy follows edits to its source's shape and can be translated by its anchor. Its bearing is fixed when created. | The kernel's rigid copy stores the initial bearing. The three-point construction uses the existing rigid endpoint for rotation. |
| Rigid Polygon preview | Fills the preview up to the controller's polygon limit. | At most 1,000 vertices can be picked or copied. The preview outlines all picks and fills at most 48 vertices. A copy must fit the 4,096-unit shape query. | Bounds vertex allocation, shape preparation and per-frame triangulation. |
| Point on Object | Handles the full set of visible paths and regions, including Cartesian curves and filled conics. | Handles pickable kernel paths and polygon or conic-part regions. Conic fills and Cartesian curves are outside the current canvas hit support. | Uses the existing canvas hit geometry. |
| Attach or Detach | Can merge a dragged point into an existing point and uses Alt to prefer a region. | Click or drag redefines the point against a supported path or region. | The desktop preserves the point's identity and its dependents. |
| Oversized shapes in Point on Object, Rigid Polygon and Relation | In the 4,097-vertex recordings, GeoGebra places a polygon-region point and a polyline-path point, creates a rigid copy with 8,195 new objects, and opens Relation for the polygon and point. | The pointer query spends at most 4,096 units on shape preparation and reads. These picks create no point or copy and cannot select the polygon for Relation. If shape work is refused and there is no hit other than a list, Point on Object and Rigid Polygon ignore the click. The numerical polygon–point comparison also refuses that shape before reading its edges. | Bounds interactive work. Recorded as `tool_point_on_large_polygon`, `tool_point_on_large_polyline`, `tool_rigid_copy_large_polygon` and `tool_relation_compares_large_polygon`. |
| Point on Object on an oversized polygon (`tool_point_on_large_polygon`) | Creates a region point in the 4,097-vertex polygon. | The pick is refused before creating a point. | The shape query allows 4,096 units, including shape preparation and vertex reads. |
| Point on Object on an oversized polyline (`tool_point_on_large_polyline`) | Creates a path point on the 4,097-vertex polyline. | The pick is refused before creating a point. | The same 4,096-unit shape query bounds the path lookup. |
| Rigid Polygon copy of an oversized polygon (`tool_rigid_copy_large_polygon`) | Creates 8,195 objects from the 4,097-vertex source. | Refuses the copy. | A copy allows at most 1,000 vertices and a 4,096-unit shape query. |
| Relation on an oversized polygon (`tool_relation_compares_large_polygon`) | Opens a polygon–point relation report for the 4,097-vertex source. | The polygon is unavailable to the pick and the relation check refuses the shape before reading its edges. | The query and comparison each cap shape work at 4,096 units. |
| Area on a polygon | Creates localized area text attached to a hidden auxiliary region point at the click. A polygon defined by named points displays those names in its caption. | The matching caption uses the desktop locale. The polygon label supplies the subject for a polygon defined from a list or with any anonymous vertices. | Placement keeps the 4,096-unit shape limit and checks at most 4,096 candidate labels. The vertex names together can use up to 4,096 bytes. |
| Area on a polygon with an occupied anchor label (`area_polygon_occupied_anchor_label`) | Renames the existing `Pointp` to `Pointp_{1}`, gives the new anchor `Pointp`, and creates `Textp`. | Keeps the existing `Pointp`, creates the anchor `Pointp_{1}`, and still creates `Textp`. | The journal has no general rename of an existing object and its references. |
| Area on a polygon that grows (`area_polygon_grows_after_placement`) | The anchor remains defined at (2, 4.51) after growth to 4,097 vertices. | The anchor becomes undefined when the region query exceeds 4,096 units and recovers its placement when the triangle fits again. | Region updates and projections use the shape budget, including preparation and vertex reads. |
| Reopen a collapsed polygon with an Area label (`area_polygon_reopen_while_collapsed`) | The anchor is at (2, 1) while collapsed. After reopening and restoring the third vertex, the anchor stays at (2, 1). | A desktop save keeps the interior parameters. The anchor returns to (2, 1.51) when the polygon regains its shape, including after reopening. | Preserves the original placement through a desktop save. |
| Outside point release (`drag_release_outside_canvas`, `outside_point_release_after_creation`) | The point is clamped to (8.92, 1.39), with zero drag checkpoints. When the previous action created B, Undo removes B. | The point is clamped to the same position. Undo first restores A to (8, 1) and keeps B. The next Undo removes B. | The owner keeps one undo step for a drag that changed the construction. |
| Return both fingers of a line drag (`two_fingers_return_line_to_start`) | The line moves from y = 2 to y = 3.19, then to y = 1.39 when both fingers return to their start pixels. | The line and both endpoints return to their exact starting positions. | Preserves the original construction when the displacement is reversed. |
| Polygon-side drag (`press_on_polygon_edge_does_nothing`) | The shared controller selects side `c` and moves its endpoints to (2.2, 0.22) and (6.2, 0.22). The third vertex stays at (1, 4.01). | The owner rule selects polygon `p` and also moves the third vertex to (2.2, 3.22). | The earlier web observation and the shared-controller capture disagree. The existing owner expectation remains asserted. |
| Rapid point double-click (`rapid_second_press_on_point`) | Opens the native redefine dialog for `A`. | Opens the definition of `A` in the algebra input row without opening the on-screen keyboard. | The desktop uses its existing definition editor. |
| Radius Escape delivery (`circle_radius_dialog_cancel`) | The shared controller opens a dialog with the centre already placed. The headless app cannot deliver Escape to Swing or the web dialog. | Escape in the desktop dialog closes it, keeps the centre, and leaves one undo step. | The capture marks where the native dialog must handle the key. |
| Single-move zoom-tool drag (`zoom_rectangle_near_view_aspect`) | The release applies click zoom at scale 75. The recorded tool stays active, so it has stored zero checkpoints. | The release applies the same click zoom and stores one checkpoint immediately. | The owner rule records a view edit as soon as it finishes. |
| Two-finger object picking | The shared controller can choose an object hit at both touches. | Object picking on a two-finger gesture is bounded to 1,024 objects. It supports two-point circles and lines or segments through two movable points. The desktop starts one undo step on the first geometry edit. The reference has zero checkpoints during the recorded circle and line moves. Axis and camera gestures remain available above the object limit. | The object query shares the normal shape hit limits and the new gesture bounds the number of objects scanned. |
| Roots and Extremum with occupied output labels | Searches for the next available point names. | Refuses the tool action if selecting the output labels requires more than 4,096 label lookups. | Bounds synchronous label search separately from the solver. |
| Create List from a rectangle (`tool_create_list_from_rectangle`) | The shared Java controller creates no list with the recorded drag. | The desktop gesture creates `l1` and can undo it. | The replay retains the desktop assertion for list creation. |
| Headless toolbar captures (`tool_text_places_literal`, `tool_input_box_places_empty_widget`) | The headless harness now submits through the reference dialog hooks and records creation and undo. | Both gestures create the corresponding object and undo it back to the initial construction. | Text and Input Box use reference replay. Create List retains the desktop assertion described above. |

## Golden corpus gap families

Each row lists the corpus IDs sharing a reason. The default ledger also covers the CAS cases that differ in builds without the Giac backend. The ledger pins the individual snapshot positions.

| Cases | Difference and reason |
| --- | --- |
| `algebralogic-logic-022` | GeoFunction.isInRegion reads the separately stored GeoPoint x2D coordinate, which can be stale at construction and after a direct headless move. The kernel evaluates the inequality at the point's current Cartesian coordinates. The construction mismatch is recorded separately in algebralogic-logic-025. |
| `algebralogic-logic-025` | GeoFunction.isInRegion also reads stale GeoPoint x2D at point construction. In the headless harness, IsInRegion((3,0), f) reads x2D = 0 for f(x) = x < 1 and reports true. The kernel evaluates f at x = 3. |
| `calculus-degree-010` | intended difference, recorded in DIFFERENCES.md: the reference exhausts its heap expanding (a x + b)^300. Its native CAS fallback returns degree zero when a is zero, but leaves the other snapshots undefined. The kernel shares the coefficient subtrees and evaluates each once, so all snapshots get a degree. At 1:f the file records null at x = -8 and x = 8, where the kernel evaluates (2x + 1)^300 to positive infinity. The file cannot be recorded again with signed samples, because the reference runs out of heap expanding the power for Degree (Function.expandToPolyFunctionNoCas) before the case ends. infinity-outputs-031 records the same function without the Degree line, with positive infinity at x = -8 and x = 8 for a = 2. |

| `cas-numeric-003` | The default build returns "Numeric requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference fallback prints 1 and \mathbf{1}. |
| `cascell-closing-004` | Kernel-only CAS rows refuse NSolutions with a starting value (text ?, LaTeX \mathbf{?}). The reference prints {1.41} and \mathbf{ \left\{ 1.41 \right\} }. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:131. |
| `cascell-lastcommands-001` | The default build returns "Numeric requires a CAS build" with empty LaTeX (crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:62). GeoGebra prints 9 and \mathbf{9}. |
| `cascell-lastround-006` | The CAS row reader rejects the comparison before cloning a middle operand that would exceed the 10,000-node limit (crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/syntax.rs:111). The kernel prints ? and \mathbf{?}. GeoGebra prints false and \mathbf{false}. |
| `cascell-stackclose-001` | The default build returns "Numeric requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints 150 and \mathbf{150}. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `cascell-stackclose-002` | The default build returns "Numeric requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints 0.13 and \mathbf{0.13}. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `cascell-stackclose-003` | The default build returns "Numeric requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints 0 and \mathbf{0}. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `cascell-stackclose-004` | The default build returns "Numeric requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints 1 * 10²⁰ and \mathbf{1 \cdot 10^{20}}. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `cascell-stackclose-005` | The default build returns "Assume requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints {-3 / 4 π, 1 / 4 π} and \mathbf{ \left\{ \frac{-3}{4} \; \pi , \frac{1}{4} \; \pi  \right\} }. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `cascell-stackclose-006` | The default build returns "Numeric requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints 9 and \mathbf{9}. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `cascell-stackclose-007` | The default build returns "ExtendedGCD requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints {1, 0, ?} and \mathbf{ \left\{ 1, 0, ? \right\} }. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `cascell-stackclose-008` | The default build returns "Eigenvalues requires a CAS build" and empty LaTeX. The reference prints ? and \mathbf{?}. See crates/geo-kernel/src/expr/cas/row.rs:63. |
| `casview-closing-001` | The reference prints a:=2 and binds a, so the next row gives 3. The single-row evaluator prints ? and leaves a + 1 symbolic. CAS assignment state and its construction twins are outside the row API. |
| `casview-closing-007` | The reference collapses 4,095 spaces to one. The desktop keeps the typed string and agrees on the result 3. |
| `casview-closing-008` | The reference trims the 4,096-byte row to one digit and keeps the repeated input-reference result 4 through an edit. Preserving the padding makes the expanded row exceed 16 KiB and print ?. |
| `casview-closing-009` | The reference collapses the saved input to one digit and a single trailing space. The importer preserves all 16,385 bytes and marks the row unevaluated with the same saved output 1. |
| `corners-textbounds-006` | when the text anchor becomes undefined, GeoGebra keeps the drawable corner at its previous position; the kernel has no stale value for an undefined parent (W1) |
| `curvepairs-bounded-001` | The symbolic solution puts the positive integer root in the first slot. The kernel orders parameters ascending, so it puts that root in the second slot even when the negative root is outside the curve domain. |
| `curvepairs-growth-001` | The reference orders the positive integer root first when the height reaches one. The kernel orders the real solutions by ascending first parameter. |
| `curvepairs-independent-001` | The symbolic system solver orders the parameter equations differently from ascending first parameter. The numerical coordinates agree for the two roots, and the labels are exchanged. |
| `curvepairs-order-001` | The symbolic system returns the four integer roots in descending first-parameter order. The numerical solver orders them ascending. |
| `curvepairs-parabola-001`, `curvepairs-parabola-002` | The symbolic solution returns the positive integer root before the negative one. The numerical solver orders them by ascending first parameter, including an undefined slot when the earlier parameter falls outside the curve domain. |
| `curvepairs-radical-001` | The reference solves the square-root coordinate symbolically. The unseeded solver requires two polynomial coordinate pairs. |
| `curvepairs-radical-002`, `curvepairs-radical-003`, `curvepairs-radical-004`, `curvepairs-radical-005` | The symbolic solution orders irrational roots negative first and switches to positive first at height four. The numerical solver orders all real solutions by ascending first parameter. |
| `discrete-path-004` | Identity-hash iteration in SparseMultigraph can select either equal-cost route through the diamond across recomputations. The kernel settles tied endpoints by first occurrence in the segment list. |
| `discrete-tree-003` | UndirectedSparseMultigraph iteration can change which equal-weight edges Prim selects, and the HashSet of TreeLink edges uses identity hashes for emission order. Both orders can vary across rebuilds. The kernel uses input-pair order. |
| `discrete-tree-005` | The reference emits the two spanning edges of the copied point list in HashSet iteration order, which depends on the identity hashes of the TreeLink edges. The kernel uses input-pair order. The single-point state and zero-length edges agree. |
| `discrete-tree-007` | The reference emits both spanning edges of the repeated-point list in HashSet iteration order, which depends on the identity hashes of the TreeLink edges. The kernel uses input-pair order. |
| `discrete-upstream-019` | AlgoDelauneyTriangulation takes Cartesian input coordinates and TriangleDt.circumcircleContains tests squared distances with a strict comparison. MyDouble.cos calls Math.cos. The recorded cos(6) is 0x1.eb9b7097822f6p-1, also returned by StrictMath and by Math.cos on the x86_64 test JVM with its libm intrinsic disabled. The warmed intrinsic and the kernel libm both return 0x1.eb9b7097822f5p-1. Substituting only the recorded coordinate reproduces all nine ordered edges. The recording depends on how the JVM evaluates the cosine, so there is no portable Math.cos result to port. Literal cocircular sites exercise the exact tie rule separately. |
| `expr-decimal-027` | intended difference, recorded in DIFFERENCES.md: aligning 1E-100001 with 1 needs 100002 digits, above the kernel's 100000-digit result limit. The remaining binary64 operations leave a rounding residual which multiplication by 1E20 turns into 5551.115123125783. GeoGebra uses BigDecimal addition and subtraction and its result rounds to 0 |
| `funcrest-derivative-004` | The 260-point spline system is solved with the reference elimination. Its y inputs come from MyDouble.sin, which calls Math.sin; on the recording JVM (HotSpot x86_64, OpenJDK 17) that call runs the libm intrinsic stub, which differs by one ulp from StrictMath (fdlibm, the kernel's algorithm) for k = 18, 24, 26, 62, 89, 131, 202 and 233 in 1..260. The ill-conditioned 260-piece monomial system amplifies these ulps to a relative 1.5e-9 in y(0.875). Black-box check: substituting the intrinsic's Math.sin values for those inputs makes the case agree, and with -XX:-UseLibmIntrinsic the JVM's Math.sin equals StrictMath.sin. The stub is HotSpot assembly under GPLv2 with no EUPL or published description, so the kernel does not port it. |
| `funcrest-splinelong-003` | Platform rounding of Math.pow in the spline matrix. The kernel computes the integer powers of the knots correctly rounded; the recording JVM (HotSpot x86_64, OpenJDK 17) runs Math.pow as its libm intrinsic stub, which is off by one ulp from that for 24 entries of this 120-point quartic system (for example (1/119)^3). The ill-conditioned system amplifies those ulps beyond 1e-9 in the curve and its derivative. Black-box check: substituting the intrinsic's Math.pow values for all 3963 powers the kernel's spline code asks for makes the case agree. The stub is HotSpot assembly under GPLv2 with no EUPL or published description, so the kernel does not port it. The later steps, including the stale point-count check, agree. |
| `geomclip-difference-001` | The contours agree geometrically. GeoGebra's sweep selects a different first vertex, which rotates the polygon vertices and the emitted point and segment slots. |
| `geomclip-difference-002` | The contours agree geometrically. GeoGebra's sweep selects a different first vertex for the subject-only contour, which rotates the polygon vertices and the emitted point and segment slots. |
| `geomclip-difference-006` | The symmetric difference contours agree geometrically. GeoGebra's sweep selects different first vertices for the right and lower contours, which rotates the emitted point and segment slots. |
| `geomclip-polygon-003` | The contours agree geometrically. After the right boundaries cross, GeoGebra's sweep starts the intersection at the crossing and the kernel starts at the top of the contour. |
| `georestclip-overlay-001` | The polygon overlay traces contours with the interior on the left and chooses its own start vertex. Labelled vertices and edges follow that order. |
| `georestclip-overlaylabels-001` | For the same area-2 diamond, GeoGebra starts the contour at (1, 0) and the kernel starts at (0, 1). This rotates the labelled output vertices and edges by one position, as documented for IntersectPath(polygon, polygon) in DIFFERENCES.md. |
| `georestsmall-matrixcurve-005` | The reference simplifies the transformed expression symbolically; the kernel uses plain arithmetic as on main. ApplyMatrix with the projective matrix {{1, 0, 0}, {0, n, 0}, {0, 0, 1}} on Curve(t, sqrt(t), t, -1, 1): GeoCurveCartesian.matrixTransform builds each coordinate with ExpressionNode.multiply and plus, which turn a zero entry into the constant itself and leave out a zero addend. For t < 0, where sqrt(t) is NaN, the recording keeps x = t finite, and y = 0 while n = 0. Plain arithmetic computes 0 * NaN = NaN in x, y and the denominator. |
| `georestsmall-matrixcurve-006` | The reference simplifies the transformed expression symbolically; the kernel uses plain arithmetic as on main. The projective row (-1, 0, 0) applied to Curve(0, t, t, -1, 1): the reference multiplies the literal 0 coordinate into -0 and leaves out the zero addends, so the denominator is -0 and the recording has negative infinity in both coordinates. Plain arithmetic adds the +0 terms, gets a +0 denominator and positive infinity. The chain e through a zero row agrees. |
| `georestsmall-matrixfunction-002` | The reference simplifies the transformed expression symbolically; the kernel uses plain arithmetic as on main. ApplyMatrix({{1, 0}, {1, 2}}, f) for f = Function(sin(x) + x(A), -1, 2): outside its domain f is NaN, and the reference builds x = t * 1 + f(t) * 0 with the zero term left out, so the signed recording keeps x = t finite. Plain arithmetic computes 0 * NaN = NaN, so x is NaN. Main compared this case with its schema 2 recording, which wrote the whole coordinate pair as null. |
| `georestsmall-surdcomplex-001` | The kernel has no complex point mode, so the input point cannot be constructed. |
| `implicit-pair-009` | The reduced Sylvester eliminant has nearly repeated roots near y = ±0.930605. The reference projects the real parts of its complex roots and polishes pairs to a 1e-8 residual. The kernel refines projected roots differently and uses up to 16 Newton iterations toward a 1e-13 residual before accepting contacts at 1e-8. It still produces four slots, including for the offset -1e-10 where the exact curves are disjoint, but the near-singular Jacobian makes their coordinates differ by more than 1e-9. |
| `keptvalues-pointgrowth-003` | The journal owns the group by its first output label. Deleting P invalidates A's recorded slot, although GeoGebra keeps A at (3, 4). Keeping A live requires a growth-group owner independent of P. |
| `keptvalues-pointpath-001` | The list literal rejects the inline CircumcircleArc. A list of paths also needs stable element identities and a composite path parameter, which the coordinate-row Point machinery does not store. |
| `locus-samplingbudget-002` | GeoGebra completes the finite diagonal locus with 149 samples. The kernel returns L undefined after repeated evaluation of the 200,000-element Sequence exhausts the shared 80,000,000-unit sampling meter (crates/geo-kernel/src/algo/locus.rs:1578). |
| `locusequation-classic-001`, `locusequation-classic-002`, `locusequation-classic-005`, `locusequation-classic-006`, `envelope-classic-001` | The kernel's curve is the reference's up to the overall sign. The reference takes the sign from its CAS's elimination of the Botana system (`locusEqu`), whose polynomial and variable lists come in hash-set order: the recorded inputs of `locusequation-midpoint-001` step 2 and `locusequation-classic-005` both have the curve variables v9 and v10, yet every coefficient sign is opposite, so no fixed variable order decides it. The kernel keeps its own normalization, a positive coefficient on the largest power of x (then of y). |
| `locusequation-classic-008` | The traced point is an intersection of a line and a circle that are both drawn through the mover. The reference excludes the mover from that intersection with an extra equation (its recorded system has t*&#124;MP&#124;^2 = 1), but only for the indexed `Intersect(a, b, n)`; the locus-equation translator omits that equation, leaving the curve undefined. |
| `matrixdilate-dilateleaves-001` | GeoGebra allocates a dilated polygon as a list element. The kernel's expression list slots cannot store the transformed polygon vertices, so the dilation is undefined. |
| `matrixdilate-dilateleaves-002` | GeoGebra allocates a dilated Cartesian curve as a list element. The kernel's expression list slots cannot store the transformed curve definition and parameter interval, so the dilation is undefined. |
| `misc-alias-003` | The contours agree geometrically. Once the moved vertex makes the triangles overlap, GeoGebra's sweep starts the intersection at the crossing of the boundaries and the kernel starts at the top of the contour, which also moves the first output point; the same difference as geomclip-polygon-003. |
| `misc-optimize-009` | The point-path locus holds the current optimum and agrees through the final wrap (a move-to). The recording then has two line-to samples at exactly the same coordinates, 151 samples against 149. AlgoLocusND inserts a closing point if the next step passes the start and the point is close, then inserts the restored point only if its coordinates differ from the last insertion. GeoLocus.insertPoint appends one point per call. The extra identical append is unexplained. |
| `misc-optimize-010` | The slider locus holds the current optimum and agrees through the final wrap (a move-to). The recording then has two line-to samples at exactly the same coordinates, 152 samples against 150. AlgoLocusSliderND inserts a closing point if the next step passes the start and the point is close, then inserts the restored point only if its coordinates differ from the last insertion. GeoLocus.insertPoint appends one point per call. The extra identical append is unexplained. |
| `odesymbolic-cas-001` | The kernel has no symbolic antiderivative for sin(x) in SolveODE and no adjustable integration constant for the form without an initial point. |
| `odesymbolic-cas-002` | The kernel has no logarithmic antiderivative for 1/x in SolveODE and no adjustable integration constant for the form without an initial point. |
| `odesymbolic-cas-003` | The kernel has no symbolic solver for differential equations whose slope expression reads y. The supplied initial points therefore do not select particular solutions. |
| `odesymbolic-cas-004` | The kernel has no symbolic solver for a two-variable slope function and cannot compute the general or initial-value solution. |
| `odesymbolic-cas-005` | The kernel has no piecewise symbolic integration for a conditional slope in SolveODE. |
| `odesymbolic-cas-006` | The polynomial solver cannot integrate a slope with a noninteger power during slider edits. The reference returns a defined symbolic function for those powers. |
| `odesymbolic-nocas-001` | intended difference, recorded in DIFFERENCES.md: the reference returns undefined for symbolic SolveODE when its CAS is disabled. The kernel integrates supported polynomial slopes directly, so both forms are defined when a is nonzero. |
| `optimizerreview-locus-004` | GeoGebra samples L as soon as the lower endpoint of s clamps it, while t's upper endpoint is still 4. The kernel resolves the slider values as each endpoint changes, then visits Q and L once in dependency order, after t's endpoint has reached 6. Both resample to the new endpoint when t moves. |
| `optimizerreview-mixedmover-002` | A nested optimizer final update refreshes q in GeoGebra before the point search reads its objective. The kernel refreshes optimizers on other movers after the outer search, so the trials still use q = 3. |
| `optimizerreview-redefine-008` | The upper endpoint reads its own slider through d. GeoGebra clamps a when b lowers that endpoint, both before and after reload in the recorded construction. During reload, the XML endpoint pass rejects the circular upper endpoint and retains the lower one (ConsElementXMLHandler.java:2647). The kernel has no cyclic listener edge and retains a = 3 and d = 6. |
| `optimizerreview-undefinedtransform-001` | At t = 1, GeoGebra dilates the retained undefined point slots and keeps M as the defined list {{?}, {?}}. The kernel returns a wholly undefined M because expr/eval.rs:1912 drops the undefined source's shape before dilation reads it (expr/eval.rs:1728). |
| `prove-expression-012`, `prove-expression-014`, `prove-expression-020`, `prove-expression-021` | `ProveDetails` of an arithmetic statement: the reference words the conditions from its CAS's factorization of the rewritten polynomial divided by the user's formula, read back through `NDGDetector`'s formula parser. The kernel decides `Prove` (the truth does not depend on the CAS's normalization) but answers an undefined list here. |
| `prove-expression-019` | The reference uses the CAS factorization and NDGDetector's formula parser to report the symbolic condition s = t in both forms of ProveDetails. The kernel returns an undefined list for each form. |
| `prove-focalconic-001` | The focus-and-point ellipse adapter uses four auxiliary focal distances, four squared-distance equations and one equation equating the sums of the distances. The prover has no translation for that adapter or its substitution at the intersection, so Prove and ProveDetails are undefined. |
| `prove-focalconic-002` | The focus-and-point hyperbola adapter uses four auxiliary focal distances, four squared-distance equations and one equation equating the differences of the distances. The prover has no translation for that adapter or its substitution at the intersection, so Prove and ProveDetails are undefined. |
| `prove-peer-001` | The reference's peer choice depends on identity-hash iteration order. This recording returns false and {false}; the kernel reports true on components. |
| `prove-recoveryundo-001` | Undoing the move restores a radius-1 circle. GeoGebra rebuilds p_2 as true. The kernel replays its creation while the circle is undefined and leaves p_2 undefined after the snapshot restore (workbench/mod.rs:636, kernel.rs:4043). Fresh proof replay needs ordered helper history and an aggregate solver budget. |
| `prove-recoveryundo-002` | Undoing the move restores a radius-1 circle. GeoGebra rebuilds the proof of the inline-line intersection as true. The kernel replays its creation while the circle is undefined and leaves p_2 undefined after the snapshot restore (workbench/mod.rs:636, kernel.rs:4043). Fresh proof replay needs ordered helper history and an aggregate solver budget. |
| `prove-relation-001`, `prove-relation-002` | intended difference, recorded in DIFFERENCES.md: the relation-tool form of ProveDetails returns an undefined list when the reference supplies a localized condition sentence. |
| `prove-replay-003` | GeoGebra replays the unaffected surviving proof before the pair is labelled and caches the indexed output's common-point exclusion. The kernel clears the cache and only recomputes proofs in the edited object's dependency graph. Replaying independent proofs in order to reseed their shared helpers requires construction-wide proof history and a replay budget. |
| `spreadsheet-filldistinctbudget-001` | GeoGebra fills A1 with ? and B1 with 0. geokernel rejects the entire fill before writing because the shifted formulas and destinations visit more than 1,024 distinct cells. See DIFFERENCES.md. |
| `spreadsheet-fillslider-001` | GeoGebra fills A1 with 7 and replaces slider B1 with 8. The kernel requires an existing slider to keep its bounds on replacement and rejects the fill before writing A1. |
| `spreadsheet-labelledfill-001` | GeoGebra writes text to A1 and 9 to B1. The kernel refuses to change A1 from a number to text because the labelled result reads it as a number. A1 stays 3, B1 remains absent, and no result is created. |
| `stroke-limits-001` | intended difference, recorded in DIFFERENCES.md: GeoGebra keeps a defined stroke with 32,489 smoothed points. The kernel marks it undefined at creation because the exact coordinates exceed the 1 MiB limit for a saved XML attribute and cannot round-trip through its importer |

## Verified t-pairs gap families

The IDs identify steps in the verified recording. Unsupported commands have no applicable kernel dispatch for the recorded call. Application commands require the editor, scripting dispatcher or view state that this construction replay does not invoke. Text-formatting gaps compare GeoGebra's recorded template against the replay printer. Incomplete-context entries require unrecorded test setup, settings or intervening mutations to compare. CAS entries need the indicated symbolic command or the recorded stand-in's behavior.

Each cascade names a recorded producer of the missing label. A failed inline operand is a form limitation, even when an unrelated step failed earlier in the method.

| `commands/CommandsTest.java:1633` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `Extremum[ sin(x), 1, 7 ]`, GeoGebra returns `(1.5708, 1)`, `(4.71239, -1)`. The interval minimum uses a different numerical search. Its x coordinate is 4.712388980384689 in the kernel and 4.712388973431372 in GeoGebra, outside the tolerance even though both round to 4.71239. |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:1649` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `Factors[ x^2 ]`, GeoGebra returns `?`. Recorded without a CAS, where Factors of a polynomial is undefined; the kernel's polynomial CAS answers {{x, 2}}. |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:1822` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `Frequency[ {"GeoGebra","rocks"},{"X","Y"} ]`, GeoGebra returns `{{1, 0}, {0, 1}}`. Frequency of two lists of texts is a contingency table in GeoGebra; the kernel's Frequency reads two lists as class boundaries and data, numbers only, and is undefined here. |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:2282` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `IntersectPath(t1,-3x+2y=10)`, GeoGebra returns `1.24808`. The reference creates a `GeoSegment` here, the kernel has no object with this label. |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:2704` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `intervalMax`, GeoGebra returns `NaN`. The kernel leaves the interval bound at 3 because it cannot run the preceding SetValue. GeoGebra has an undefined interval by this step, so Max is undefined. |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:3734` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `Roots[ sin(x), 4, 13 ]`, GeoGebra returns `(6.283185305816606, 0)`, `(9.424777959654795, 0)`, `(12.566370613845491, 0)`. The values agree within the tolerance, but a numerically polished root differs from the reference's in its last digits (6.28318530725133 against 6.283185305816606), so the full-precision strings cannot agree. |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:4044` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `A`, GeoGebra returns `(3, 3)`. Kernel replay skips the preceding SetCoords call, leaving `A` at (1, 1). GeoGebra changes `A` to (3, 3). |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:4052` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `A`, GeoGebra returns `(Infinity, 0)`. Kernel replay skips SetCoords with an infinite x coordinate and leaves the point on the x-axis at (0, 0). The reference moves it to (Infinity, 0). |
| `commands/CommandsTest.java:4056` | **kernel-mismatch.** With `B`, GeoGebra returns `(2, Infinity)`. Kernel replay skips SetCoords with an infinite y coordinate and leaves the point on the line at (2, 0). The reference moves it to (2, Infinity). |

## Import gap families

An object is identified by its case ID and label, joined by `@`. The entries pin saved conic classification and tangency differences. The five line distances use the recorded reference reload as their import expectation.

| Cases | Difference and reason |
| --- | --- |
| `saved-doubletangent-001@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-002@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-003@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-004@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-005@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-006@P_{2}` | Line and circle reach exact tangency after both outputs receive labels. The indexed solver suppresses the duplicate. |
| `saved-doubletangent-007@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-008@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-009@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-010@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-011@P_{2}`, `saved-doubletangent-012@P_{2}` | Two circles reach exact tangency after both outputs receive labels. The indexed solver suppresses the duplicate. |
| `transform-dilate-048@n` | n = Dilate(m, 2) of the single point m (a circle of radius 8e-5 that GeoGebra classifies as a point) is a single point of radius 0 in GeoGebra and in the kernel; the saved matrix, classified afresh, is a circle of radius 1.6e-4 |
